#10539. AI Has A Trust Problem. The Answer Is In The Chip

Ayal Yogev is the CEO and co-founder of Anjuna Security.

​The release of Claude’s Mythos model ​was a warning: AI can’t be ignored or fully trusted. At least not yet. ​

In April, Anthropic gave approximately 50 companies that build or maintain critical software access to Mythos, its new model, through Project Glasswing. Within weeks, Mythos found a 27-year-old OpenBSD vulnerability, plus a bug in FFmpeg that five million automated tests had missed.

While it was a boon for defenders, the same capability cuts both ways. Just a month later, Google’s Threat Intelligence Group confirmed the first known zero-day exploit built with AI help.​​

Both cases point to the same alarming shift: The time to find a security flaw and the time to exploit a zero-day vulnerability have both collapsed to machine speed. ​Two forces explain why:

The Zero Day Clock measures the time-to-exploit (TTE) window from common vulnerabilities and exposures disclosure to active exploitation. In 2018, the median time stood at 771 days. By 2025, that window plummeted to the point where the majority of vulnerabilities were weaponized by bad actors before they were disclosed.​

Models like Mythos have shifted the risk balance even further in the attackers’ favor by exposing more vulnerabilities than can be timely patched.​​

In the datacenter era, discovering and exploiting vulnerabilities both required manual effort. Cloud and SaaS followed and only automated the exploitation phase. Now, AI has automated both steps, effectively closing the response window.

The second force involves who holds insider access.​

Early enterprise computing granted privileged access to a small set of employees. Cloud and SaaS extended that to vendor engineers with standing credentials. Today, AI agents hold access to systems and data on behalf of those users. ​

Many assume AI agents have limited access to sensitive data, but the opposite is usually true. That shows up in three ways:​

• Non-Human Identity Sprawl: Agents need their own lifecycle in provisioning, least-privilege scoping, monitoring and decommissioning.

• Data-In-Use Exposure: The moment an agent reads or processes data is a new, largely ungoverned trust boundary.

• Third-Party Model Dependency: Sending data to an external model provider extends the insider boundary beyond the organization’s own infrastructure.

In customer environments, seemingly benign coding agents reach into users’ local environments, read files and environment data and pass that content back to the model.

In one instance I saw, when sensitive data was blocked from reaching the model, the agent hallucinated plausible substitutes to complete the task. That fabricated data, if written back into a production system, could have introduced errors that nobody would think to trace back to an AI session. ​

A compromised agent token can give an attacker the same lateral access the agent already had, turning one compromised session into a path through the environment.​​

The two forces demand an AI-first approach to security, trust and governance, as the old assumptions and security approaches no longer hold: ​

• Perimeter and identity tools decide who gets in, but they were built for human pace instead of autonomous agents.

• Encryption protects data at rest and in motion, but the moment an agent reads it, that data sits exposed.

• Zero trust continuously checks identity, but it assumes the integrity of the underlying compute layer.

• AI governance documents intent without being able to enforce it.

• Sandboxes and gateways contain blast radius, but without a hardware root underneath, a sandbox remains a policy rather than evidence, and can be circumvented by the agents, as in the Hugging Face breach.​

In short, software protecting software is DevHopeOps, not security.​

One way to mitigate this problem is to favor protection over detection. For example, confidential computing can help keep data protected even if there is a zero-day vulnerability in the hypervisor or operating system. I should note that my company is one of several offering a confidential computing solution.

Confidential computing runs workloads in hardware-isolated, encrypted enclaves built into modern CPUs and GPUs, where applications and data stay sealed off from everyone else on the machine, including administrators, cloud operators and other software, and where the code is cryptographically attested before it touches sensitive data. ​​

Alternative technologies include Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE), where cryptography lets you run computations directly on encrypted data without decryption, and open source confidential computing solutions. However, FHE affects performance and requires modifications to the application code, while open source solutions require lots of scaffolding to make it work.​

Even with those solutions, enterprises should still scan their code for vulnerabilities that may introduce a back door for hacking. This security hygiene is table stakes against attacks.​​​

Defending against exploits at AI-speed requires specific steps today:​

• Map your exploit-lifecycle exposure. Inventory where AI-generated code, AI coding agents and AI-facing data flows already exist.

• Treat every agent as a non-human identity. Register, scope to least privilege, monitor and decommission agents with the same rigor as privileged human accounts.

• Re-examine what “patch fast” buys you. AI-speed patching is necessary but not sufficient. Pair it with controls that don’t depend on human or automated reaction time.

• Use technology that protects data in use, not just data at rest and in transit. Confidential computing, for example, is one technology category that was built for this gap.

• Demand proof, not policy, from anything claiming to supervise AI or agent behavior. Ask whether the control can attest to its own integrity, or whether it just assumes a trusted environment beneath it.​

• Watch the access model of frontier AI security tools as an indicator of how the industry is managing dual-use risk. Understand what restricted models, like Mythos, and more broadly available models, like Daybreak, are capable of. Factor that into your own governance posture.​

• Continue investing in fundamentals. MFA, least privilege and visibility remain the highest-return controls even as threats accelerate.

The deciding factor isn’t whether an organization adopts AI, but whether its trust model can be verified rather than assumed.​​

#10535. Bringing The Factory To The Worker Could Solve The Skills Gap

Alexander Clausbruch, Radix NA CEO, enabling customers from Vision to Value with operational excellence and strong, sustainable results.

Today’s global headlines frequently focus on AI eliminating jobs. But the opposite is often true in heavy industry: companies simply cannot find enough workers to fill the roles.​

In manufacturing and energy sectors, younger workers can be reluctant to take roles in remote or offshore environments. Meanwhile, more experienced operators are retiring in droves, creating a widening skills gap.​

The numbers make for stark reading. In advanced economies, 2.4 energy workers are close to retiring for every worker under 25, according to the International Energy Agency. This means, between now and 2035, two out of every three new energy-sector hires will be needed just to replace retiring workers.​​

​Manufacturing is similarly up against it. According to the US Bureau of Labor Statistics, job openings for industrial mechanics and maintenance workers are projected to grow by 14% by 2035, with approximately 51,900 openings annually over the next coming decade.

These figures clearly contradict the prevailing narrative that AI is replacing heavy industry jobs. The more nuanced story is that technology can help address labor shortages by making scarce human expertise more productive. We already see this in the growth of centralized operations centers, where experts can oversee multiple facilities remotely while working closer to where they actually want to live.

Technologies like digital twins, AI and connected industrial systems mean engineers and specialists no longer need to be standing beside assets or machinery to make sense of them.​ The global mining giant Rio Tinto offers a glimpse of what this could look like at scale. Around 90% of its haul truck fleet across the Pilbara is now autonomous, while its operations center in Perth allows trucks, trains, plants and drills to be operated from 1,500 kilometers (over 900 miles) away.​

Roles that once depended on having workers physically present at remote mine sites are increasingly shifting toward remote operations specialists and autonomous fleet controllers.​ We are seeing the same trend at Radix. Leafcutter, our industrial data collection technology, reached a milestone with an oil and gas client, moving 1 billion data points per day from more than 4 million process variables at under two seconds of latency.​

Crucially, connectivity such as this means real-time operational data can be made available beyond the plant. Data scientists and external technical specialists can access information that would traditionally have been available primarily to operators and engineers working on site.​

This is also the foundation on which industrial AI depends. The value of collecting vast quantities of real-time data is not simply that people can see it remotely. AI systems can analyze that data continuously, helping identify patterns, flag emerging problems and give engineers the information they need to intervene earlier.​

None of this removes the need for people on industrial sites. Plants, factories and energy facilities will continue to require operators, engineers and maintenance workers on the ground.​ But industrial AI, digital twins and connected operations can change the relationship between people and physical assets. AI can process an increasingly complex flow of operational data, while human expertise can be deployed where judgement and intervention are most valuable.​

So, in response to today’s AI doomsayers: yes, the factory of the future will still need people in abundance. The difference is that not all of them will need to be on the factory floor.​

#10531. How to Build a Typed AI Pipeline With Jev and Clairic.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A support email lands: "Order NW-47790, the tent poles snapped on the first night, I paid $389 and I want every cent back today or I'm disputing the charge."

To act on that, your code needs answers to a handful of small questions. Is it a refund request? How urgent is it? Should a person handle it? What's the order number? How much money is involved? It's the same problem whether you're sorting a support inbox or building a custom chatbot that has to work out what each customer is asking for.

The usual move is to send the whole email to a chat model with a prompt that ends "respond in JSON", then validate whatever comes back. It works, but you're paying a model built to write essays to fill in five fields, and you're parsing its guess at a schema.

System One models are built for exactly this kind of job. In this tutorial we'll build a small support inbox pipeline with two of them: Jev from TypeSafe makes the decisions, and Clairic from Cloudflare reads the values. Plain TypeScript then turns their answers into an action. No prompt engineering for output formats, and no JSON repair.

The name comes from Daniel Kahneman's Thinking, Fast and Slow. System 1 is the fast, intuitive kind of thinking you use to read a face or a sign. System 2 is the slow, deliberate kind you use for long division.

A System One model is an AI model that understands natural language but doesn't generate text. You give it some input (the state) and a set of typed questions, and it gives back typed answers with probabilities. There's no free text to parse, because the model can only answer in the shape you asked for.

That's different from asking a chat model for structured output. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema shows how to constrain a chat model and validate its JSON with Zod. With a System One model, the type is the interface: a yes/no question comes back as a probability, a pick-one question comes back as one of your options, and a date comes back as a date.

Jev and Clairic are both System One models, but they do different jobs, and that's what makes them a good pair.

Jev can't hand you an order number, and Clairic has no way to pick from a list of options or answer yes or no. Give each one the job it's built for.

If you've read SitePoint's Jev and LangChain tutorial, you've seen Jev deciding which model handles an agent's request. Here we'll use it on the incoming text itself.

Five made-up emails for a made-up outdoor gear store go in. For each one:

Both models read the same email, so they run at the same time.

Put the three values in your environment:

No SDKs are needed. Both models are a single fetch call.

Here's the test inbox. Each email is there to test a different path through the rules:

Jev takes a state (the email) and a set of named questions. We'll ask three, one of each type:

All three questions go in one request. Jev reads the email once and answers every question in parallel, so adding a question costs very little:

We pin jev-1.14.0 rather than the jev-latest alias. Your thresholds get tuned against one version's probabilities, so you want to choose when that version changes.

For the angry tent email, Jev came back with:

(Trimmed: the real response also includes the probability of every option and level.)

Clairic takes the same state, plus a set of typed fields instead of questions:

reference_date anchors relative dates like "the 14th" or "last Monday". In production, pass the date the email arrived.

Each answer comes back with three things worth using:

For the "where is it?" email, Clairic read the deadline like this:

"The 14th" became a full date, worked out from the reference date. Text fields like order_number also come back as character spans of the original email. The value is copied from the input, not generated, so it can't invent an order number that isn't there.

The two models don't depend on each other, so send both requests together:

The time for each email is whichever model is slower, not the two added together.

This is the part a chat model usually ends up doing badly: the business rules. Here they're ordinary code you can read, test, and change without touching a prompt:

Notice that the model's confidence sits right next to the business rule. A $18.95 refund is only automatic if Clairic is sure about both the order number and the amount. Raising the refund limit or the confidence bar is a one-line change, and you don't need to call either model again to see what it would do. Save the answers and replay them.

Every email went where it should. The whole run used 2,703 input tokens on Jev and 2,292 on Clairic, about US$0.0003 in total. At that rate, 10,000 support emails would cost roughly US$0.70.

The interesting part of the run wasn't the right answers. It was the two wrong ones.

"From last Monday." The run used 2 November 2026 as the reference date, which is a Monday. "Last Monday" means 26 October, but Clairic answered 2026-11-02, the reference date itself. Its confidence on that answer was 0.54, compared with 0.99 or higher on almost every other value in the run.

"A couple of weeks ago." Clairic turned this into 2026-10-19, which is a fair reading but still a guess. Its confidence was 0.43.

Neither date changed any action, because the rules only trust values at 0.9 or above. That's the habit to build: a value under your bar counts as unknown. A chat model returning JSON would have handed you "2026-11-02" with the same certainty as everything else.

One more catch: Clairic copied the order number as #NW-48213, hash included, because that's how the customer wrote it. Text values are spans of the input, so normalising them is your code's job. That's one line of code.

System One models handle the reading and the routing. They don't hold a conversation. Once an email is routed, something still has to write the reply, ask a follow-up question, or explain a returns policy in the customer's own words. That's where a chat model earns its cost.

The split that works is: System One models decide, code applies the rules, and a chat model only gets the cases where words matter.

We built a support inbox triage pipeline from two small models and some ordinary code:

Each email took about half a second and cost less than a hundredth of a cent. Most importantly, the two answers that would have been wrong came back flagged as unsure.

The full code is about 160 lines with no dependencies. Try adding a fourth Jev question (does the customer mention a photo?) or an estimate field in Clairic (how many days until the trip?), and see how little the rest of the pipeline has to change.

Throwing 70,480 CPU cores at Azeroth so you don't have to stare at a login queue

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Blizzard pulled out all the stops to ensure World of Warcraft: Forever survived a chaotic launch night. What was the company's secret weapon? Splurging an absurd amount of money on infrastructure made up of 3,924 virtual machines with 70,480 CPU cores and a whopping 251,136 GB, or 251 TB, of memory.

Praised by media and players as one of the smoothest launches in Blizzard's history, World of Warcraft: Forever requires significant computing firepower. 3,924 virtual machines work out to 18 cores and 64GB of memory per instance. Or, the company could be using a mixture of 16-core and 32-core nodes with slightly less memory.

To put the infrastructure's scale in consumer terms, we are looking at about 8,810 mainstream processors, or 7,848 high-end gaming systems with 32GB of memory each. In any event, the hardware is greatly improved compared to what World of Warcraft used to run on back in 2009.

*Slaps Roof of WoW: Forever* This baby is running on: 3,924 Virtual Machines 251,136 GB of Memory 70,480 CPU Cores https://t.co/P9PdRBzke7November 5, 2026

In 2009, Blizzard said it used 10 data centers worldwide to host World of Warcraft. At the time, the infrastructure included 13,250 server blades, totaling 75,000 CPU cores and 112.5 TB of memory. By comparison, World of Warcraft: Forever leverages 6% fewer CPU cores but rocks 2.2X more memory. Modern processors are leagues faster than chips from 17 years ago. By today's standards, the bottleneck is memory capacity and bandwidth, which is why the infrastructure can run on fewer cores but has more than double the memory to compensate.

Blizzard does not specify the exact hardware that World of Warcraft: Forever runs on. However, if we were to translate the core specifications to, for example, AMD's EPYC 128-core Bergamo or 96-core Genoa chips, it could be anywhere between 275 and 365 2U server nodes, depending on which processor it uses. Therefore, World of Warcraft: Forever could be running on just eight to ten 42U server racks, a noteworthy improvement over 17 years ago.

Blizzard should have no trouble building the infrastructure for World of Warcraft: Forever. A ballpark estimate for about ten 42U server racks with EPYC chips is at least $15 million, even with today's memory pricing. But then again, since Microsoft owns Blizzard now, the company could just be tapping its parent's existing Azure platform. Technically, it is not free compute, but Blizzard would benefit from cost pricing and priority allocation of Azure's capacity.

World of Warcraft: Forever shows that a high-profile launch does not have to be a nightmare when companies are willing to invest in computing infrastructure. In this case, many would agree Blizzard is putting those 251TB to good use, although Sam Altman may say otherwise.

#10526. Three months in, Galaxy Z Fold 8’s cameras aren’t enough, and Fold 9 might not fix it [Gallery]

I’ve been enamored with my Galaxy Z Fold 8 since I started using it roughly three months ago, but its one big downside has been the cameras, so I’m not exactly thrilled to hear that the Galaxy Z Fold 9 might now make any progress here.

In my review of the Galaxy Z Fold 8, I called the cameras “the biggest mistake in this whole package,” and that opinion hasn’t changed after three months. The duo of 50MP sensors get the job done, but not as well as I want for such an expensive smartphone.

What I’ve found as I’ve continued to use this device that is that I don’t want to use these cameras. I’ll use them for cases where quality isn’t as important, like snapping pictures of my meals for my nutrition app, scanning documents, and things like that, but I’m always reaching for the phone in my other pocket for any other snaps. And that’s a privilege that not everyone has, nor should they be required to do so on their pricey foldable.

The downsides I’m most frequently running into include a lack of any quality zoom range – quality falls off a cliff beyond 2x – and problems with framing. The short design of the Galaxy Z Fold 8 means that, often, I’m capturing my finger at the bottom of the shot unintentionally.

The results through the main lens are perfectly fine, and I’m actually rather enjoying Samsung’s saturated colors as the Fall foliage cranks up, but I’m still constantly underwhelmed.

Foldable cameras have always been lesser compared to standard smartphones, but the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s lack of a third lens just makes that a tiny bit worse.

And yet, it sounds like Samsung is happy with the status quo. Some leakers are claiming that the Galaxy Z Fold 9 won’t make any changes to the camera sensors. A 50MP main and a 50MP ultra-wide. Fold 9 UItra might get a new main sensor, but no major changes to the hardware either.

To say that’s disappointing is an understatement, but if I can make one plea to Samsung, it’s to at least change the camera layout next time around. “Core identity” excuses aside, constantly needing to readjust my grip on the phone just to avoid taking a picture of my finger is frustrating, and having a new layout also opens the door to improvements on the table wobble and the whole Qi2 debacle. If we’re not getting new sensors, at least let’s get some physical improvements to the layout.

#10525. The Intent Gap: Why Retail’s Unified Customer View Was Never Really Unified

Rohit Kedia is a software architect turned CEO of Xoriant, a AI-native Applied Intelligence digital engineering company.

For years, retailers have invested heavily in building a unified view of their customers, bringing together interactions across channels to better understand their behavior. But in engineering over 350 platforms across Fortune 500 and Global 2000 companies, and looking closely at customer data and experience challenges, one distinction became clear: Knowing what a customer has done isn’t the same as understanding what they’re likely to do next.

The problem isn’t a lack of data. It’s the inability to connect signals across touchpoints and translate them into meaningful insights about customer intent. For retailers, bridging the intent gap can help them move beyond simply tracking customer interactions to making better-timed, more informed decisions that improve retention.​

Retail has already moved through two eras of customer intelligence. The first connected point-of-sale systems, loyalty platforms and CRM data into a single record of who the customer is and what they bought, promising that seeing every transaction would be enough to make sound decisions. The second added activity-level insights on interaction data—emails opened, pages browsed and calls logged—on the assumption that more touchpoints meant more understanding.

Per Salesforce’s 2025 State of Sales research, 87% of respondents say data silos and trapped data affect their ability to build a unified customer view,​ with more than one-third of that percentage experiencing a severe impact. Separate research by IDC found that up to 90% of enterprise data was unstructured in 2022, living in emails and transcripts rather than a CRM. That’s an architectural problem.​​​

Customers rarely announce their real motivations directly. Some intent is explicit, like a search for a specific product or a request for a quote. But much of what matters is never stated at all. These implicit motivations, revealed only through behavior, show up in price hesitation as an abandoned cart, brand fatigue as declining open rates and competitor shopping as shorter sessions, long before any of it becomes a stated preference.

Closing this “intent gap” means connecting fragmented signals to understand customer intent, uncover unmet needs and use those insights to predict behavior, take action and deliver measureable results, rather than just populate another dashboard.

This is where the context layer plays a key role. The same signal, like a customer’s login frequency dropping, can mean churn, a completed purchase, seasonality or a changed need. The surrounding context tells you which of these may be true.

AI is beginning to both expose the intent gap and hold the power to close it.

Unlike the previous two eras—identifying who the customer is and what they did—the intent era helps us understand what’s likely to happen next, why and what should be done about it. When paired with agentic AI, intent-driven intelligence runs as a continuous reasoning loop, monitoring changing conditions, investigating anomalies, testing a live hypothesis against new data rather than scoring a snapshot and surfacing a recommendation traceable back to the signals that produced it. This is a departure from most retail AI today, which is predictive—a model trained on historical data that produces a score.

Either way, true intent intelligence only creates value if it’s acted upon.

Salesforce’s 2024 Connected Shoppers research found that 89% of retailers expect returns on their AI investments, with employee productivity topping the list followed by better shopping experiences.

An intent-aware retailer can identify churn risk in the erosion of engagement that precedes it— before a complaint surfaces. They can detect buying momentum even before a cart is started. They can catch a launch or promotion drifting off course before it’s too late to regroup.

No matter where intent is spotted, the objective isn’t better customer intelligence for its own sake. It’s earlier intervention, more relevant experiences and measurable business outcomes.

Intent intelligence doesn’t replace human judgment; it amplifies it. AI can surface patterns and recommend actions at a scale no team could match, but it can’t supply the context of a relationship, the domain expertise to spot a genuine risk or the ethical oversight to act on a sensitive signal. Those remain distinctly human, making it the product of human ingenuity and AI working together.

One lesson I’ve learned is that customer data rarely tells the whole story on its own. Whether it’s a retailer that sees a loyal customer making fewer purchases and assumes they’re losing interest, or an e-commerce business that interprets repeated visits to a product page without a purchase as a lack of buying intent, the real reason may be in signals they haven’t connected.

​For retailers, interpreting these signals and closing the intent gap isn’t just another data or technology initiative. It’s about getting closer to the customer, making more informed decisions and earning the next interaction rather than taking it for granted.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

This could help Ukrainian operatives see what Russian drone operators are seeing.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

CODE380, a radio manufacturer based in Kyiv, just built a new software-defined radio (SDR) that intercepts analog video signals that Russian drones transmit to their operators, making it easier for troops to find and intercept them. According to Defense News, the D·Vision SDR can sweep radio frequencies from 500 MHz to 8.7 GHz and convert any intercepted analog radio signal into a video feed. It has a detection range of up to six miles for signals on the 540 MHz to 6 GHz band, and up to two to three miles for higher frequencies.

Although this isn’t the first passive Ukraine-made drone detector, it’s one of the lightest models produced so far, weighing just 10 ounces or 290 grams — by comparison, an iPhone 18 Pro Max weighs about 8.78 ounces or 249 grams. Its competitors include the Vydun 7.5, which weighs around 2 pounds or 900 grams and could intercept signals at up to 7.5 GHz; the 23-ounce (650-gram) Chuyka 3.0, which can detect signals at up to 8.8 GHz; and the SENSE-4, which weighs 21 ounces or 600 grams and could detect drone signals of up to 8.2 GHz.

All these receivers cover the 4.1 to 4.5 GHz band that Russian Molniya strike drones began using in June of this year to transmit video back to their operators, Presidential advisor Serhii “Flash” Beskrestnov said. However, he also warned against relying on them for protection alone. “Most detectors don’t see these frequencies, or see them poorly,” he said in a social media post. “I’m warning you so that trust in a detector doesn’t cost you your life.” These receivers are often designed to work in tandem with other sensors, like radar, acoustic sensors, and drone interceptors, as part of an anti-drone defense system.

Most of these units are designed to intercept analog signals, meaning they won't be useful for intercepting video signals once Russia figures out how to use digital signals. Still, the D·Vision SDR is claimed to have digital UAV detection, although it’s only tuned towards DJI drones for now. It’s unclear what types of radios Russian drones use, though, so the company would have to recover downed units to inspect their hardware and build appropriate countermeasures. It will also be ineffective against fiber optic drones, which are both jam- and intercept-proof as they use a physical connection to communicate with their home base.

this is super cool! i guess my first question is if it is subject to the Android 32 subprocess limit that effectively cripples the terminal on Googlebooks.

#10520. Porting the NDK “Endless Tunnel” to iOS: A Masterclass in Cross-Platform C++

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When developers talk about high-performance mobile games, the Android NDK samples by Bruno Oliveira are often cited as the gold standard for clean, low-level C++. But as an iOS developer, I wanted to see if I could take that same “pure” C++ logic and make it sing on an iPhone.

My latest project, TunnelFeveriOS, is the result of that journey. It isn’t just a port; it’s a blueprint for building a custom game engine.

The original Endless Tunnel is built to live within the Android lifecycle (JNI, Activity, and NDK). Moving this to iOS required more than just hitting “compile.” I had to build a Platform Abstraction Layer.

To make the C++ engine talk to the iOS UI, I utilized Objective-C++ (.mm** files)**. This allowed me to keep the game's high-speed math and geometry in native C++ while using Cocoa Touch to manage the windowing and touch input. In TunnelFeveriOS, you’ll see how the view controller manages the game loop, feeding time deltas and touch coordinates directly into the C++ core.

Since the original game uses OpenGL ES, I leveraged iOS’s GLKit framework. The trick was ensuring that the shaders and vertex buffer objects (VBOs) originally written for Android were correctly initialized within the iOS EAGLContext. The result? 60 FPS performance that feels native because, at the machine level, it is.

A port is only “production-ready” if it handles modern app requirements. I integrated Google Mobile Ads (AdMob) to trigger Interstitial ads during game-over states.

This presented a unique threading challenge:

I implemented a messaging system that allows the C++ core to “request” an ad, which the Objective-C wrapper then catches and executes on the main thread — preventing the game from hanging or crashing.

This project proves that you don’t need a heavy engine like Unity or Unreal to achieve cross-platform results. By keeping the “brain” of your game in C++ and building thin “shells” for each platform (Android NDK or iOS/XCode), you gain:

If you’re interested in how to bridge the gap between low-level C++ and high-level iOS development, check out the full source code here:

Now that the foundation is laid, I’m looking into implementing a Metal-based renderer to fully embrace the future of Apple graphics. If you’ve worked on C++ to Metal bridges, I’d love to hear your thoughts in the comments!

#10519. Stop Training Everyone: Why The Highest Return On Enterprise AI Comes From The 60% You’re Ignoring

David Mainiero, Chief AI Officer at AI Digital, leads AI Digital Labs to embed practical, workflow-ready AI into media and marketing.

​At AI Digital, more than 600 people have access to advanced AI platforms, including knowledge-work agents, coding tools and agentic workflows. We’ve invested millions in training, upskilling and internal-tool development. Employees can also request API budgets to build with models beyond our core enterprise subscriptions.​

If any organization should have uniform adoption, it’s ours. It doesn’t.

​About 18% of our organization are power users. One engineer can ship applications that once required a four-person team. A strategist can test more campaign variations in a week than an entire team previously produced in a month. Conservatively, our roughly 110 power users generate the productive equivalent of more than 300 additional employees.

​The temptation is to make everyone a power user. But training alone won’t do that. Power users tend to self-select. The organization’s role is to remove obstacles: provide compute and autonomy, establish sensible guardrails and recruit people with that disposition.

​At the other end are near-zero users, roughly 20% of the organization. Tools are available, and training has been offered, yet usage remains minimal. Some roles have fewer high-value AI use cases; others lack a compelling application or have been given tools that assume too much technical fluency. Beyond a certain point, more training costs more than redesigning the workflow around the user.

​Then, there’s the middle: 62% of the organization. They use AI to summarize, draft and answer basic questions, but haven’t used agentic workflows or seen how a well-designed AI system can take on complex work.

​This group contains the richest untapped leverage. There are nearly four times as many of them as power users. Move the middle from marginal use to even a modest productivity multiplier, and an organization can create more aggregate capacity than its entire power-user cohort generates.

​The distribution will vary by company. But 20/60/20 is a useful directional rule of thumb, and the highest marginal return on structured AI investment is often in the middle.​

The first phase of enterprise AI was about teaching people to use AI. The next is about designing systems that don’t require every employee to become an expert.​

​Each step should require less AI sophistication from the end user while enabling more sophisticated work. People in the middle aren’t incapable of advanced AI-enabled work; too often, they’ve simply been given an interface that asks too much of them.

​Build shared skills: reusable prompt architectures and workflows within an enterprise AI environment. A media planner shouldn’t need to master prompt engineering. They need a tool that accepts familiar inputs and produces a deliverable they can assess and refine.

​The AI generates the first complete output. The human supplies judgment, context, exceptions and accountability. The last-mile capabilities—evaluating output, redirecting it and applying domain expertise to finish the work—can then be taught in hours, not months. That’s where much of the training budget belongs.

​Most enterprise AI dashboards emphasize activity: activation rates, monthly active users and prompts per user. Those measures can rise without business transformation.

​The more important metric is graduation rate: How many casual users from last quarter became meaningfully more capable this quarter?

​A useful barometer is when middle-tier users begin requesting API budgets, when noncoders start building or when someone who was summarizing emails three months earlier begins assembling tools. That’s a graduation event—and it tells you that their work has changed.

​We track who’s moving beyond casual use and design enablement programs to accelerate it. For example, our AI Experience Center in Miami, scheduled to open in October, is designed for the middle group. It makes the future of media and advertising relevant to their work. The goal isn’t simply education. It’s epiphany. When people see what’s possible in a relevant context, belief follows—and belief drives behavior change.​

Many AI strategies overlook managers. You can give employees access to capable models, but if deliverables, approval processes and staffing models remain unchanged, and nobody identifies tasks that can be delegated to agents, employees will use AI as a faster spellchecker.

​The middle rarely graduates on inspiration alone. Managers must redesign the work. The true unit of AI transformation isn’t the individual employee—it’s the workflow.

​The model is straightforward: Power users discover capabilities, the organization turns what they learn into shared skills and tools, the middle adopts them with minimal friction and some become power users who discover the next wave. The bottom group receives baseline access and clear expectations without consuming disproportionate transformation resources.​

​Stop trying to turn every employee into an AI expert. Build an organization in which they don’t need to be one.

#10516. Homelabber uses second-gen Optane memory sticks to run a 2.8-trillion-parameter AI model on CPU alone

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

It’s slow, it’s loud, but it runs on old Optane.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A homelab builder on Reddit has run Moonshot AI’s 2.8-trillion-parameter Kimi K3 AI model on discontinued second-gen Optane PMem 200 memory, using a 40-core 3rd-gen Xeon Scalable CPU and not a GPU for inference. The build runs hot, and the builder’s advice is that you “treat active DIMM cooling as mandatory,” with dual 12W blower fans aimed at the modules to get temperatures under control. The project, a follow-up to a separate user’s first-gen Optane build earlier this year, runs at a sluggish 0.85 tokens per second even with 1-bit quantization.

Kimi K3 is absolutely not built for home use, in our estimation, with Moonshot AI recommending data center inference engines to run it, and even the smallest community quantization needs at least 610GB of RAM. Using secondhand Optane, instead, is one of the few ways to reach that capacity at home. It doesn’t help that DRAM prices have skyrocketed, and the builder says the Optane memory costs “a fraction” of the equivalent.

Intel launched its Optane Persistent Memory 200 Series (PMem 200) in 2020 for its 3rd-gen Xeon Scalable CPUs in 128GB, 256GB, and 512GB capacities. On two-socket Xeon platforms, it runs at a higher effective MT/s rate than the first generation (PMem 100) that was used on the first project, and it can deliver an average of up to 32% more memory bandwidth. Optane can run in either Memory Mode or App Direct Mode; both builds had platforms allowing for each.

Memory Mode has the Optane act as system memory with DRAM as its cache, the method used in the first build, while App Direct exposes Optane as a separate, persistent region that the software maps directly, the mode used in the second. This can give “more control over where the model’s tensors live,” according to the first build’s creator, u/APFrisco, who planned to try it.

Performance does take a hit by going with Optane over DRAM, and with the higher 3-bit quantization, the speed went down even more to 0.75 tokens per second. One commenter points out that this is very slow, with a 50k-token task estimated to take 13 hours at about 1 token per second, but another notes that overnight batches don’t care as much about speed. Both speeds compare to the first build’s roughly 4 tokens per second, although in that case the builder had an RTX 3060 handling part of the model in addition to Memory Mode, a 2-bit quantization, and the smaller, earlier 1-trillion-parameter K2.5 model.

The latest builder also had to deal with heat output, with the Optane DIMMs getting “extremely hot” with sustained inference, creating a situation the builder says can endanger neighboring DRAM. That heat is consistent with Intel’s own specs, as the manufacturer rates the modules with forced airflow of 2.4 to 2.7 meters per second for cooling, which is what you expect to find in data centers. You might not normally have this level of airflow pointed directly at your memory sticks.

The expected operating conditions put each Optane module at 12W or 15W, regardless of capacity. If the build used four sticks, that would mean up to 60W of heat placed right next to the system memory. Throttling on these occurs at 83 degrees Celsius, with a thermal trip shutdown at 85 degrees, compared to the 85 degrees Celsius and up to 95 degrees in an extended range that typical DDR4 server memory is rated for. A lot of heat has to be removed from the system to keep things running.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The performance numbers given are pre-tuning — this means without tuning thread counts or NUMA affinity — so there is room for improvement, and one commenter also asked about using Memory Mode with DRAM caching for the most-used experts. The builder plans to add GPUs and to also load parts of the model into DRAM as a faster tier alongside the Optane, with “more benchmarks to come.”

#10510. How Product Teams Can Use AI User Research to Test Assumptions Before Sprint Planning

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sprint planning is on Thursday, and the top of the backlog rests on three things nobody has checked. Users want the feature, they will find it, and they will understand what it does when they do. Each belief is plausible, and each has already been written into a ticket.

AI user research uses language models to simulate or analyze user conversations, so a team can put a question to a modeled audience in minutes instead of recruiting people for weeks. Used well, it gives a product team a cheap first check on backlog assumptions. Used badly, it produces confident-sounding agreement. This guide covers a two-day routine that gets the benefit and avoids the trap.

An assumption is worth testing when the plan depends on it and a wrong answer would change the ticket. "Users like clean interfaces" fails that test. "Analysts re-apply the same filters every session" passes, because if it is false the saved-filters feature shrinks or disappears.

The timing matters because planning leaves little room for research. The Scrum Guide says Sprint Planning is timeboxed to a maximum of eight hours for a one-month Sprint, and the first question the team answers is why the Sprint is valuable. Checks run before that meeting inform the answer. Checks run during it eat the time meant for deciding.

Write each risky item as a short card that names the belief, the consequence of being wrong, and the decision it feeds. A card forces the team to say what would change its mind, which most tickets never do. Here is an example:

Aim for three to five cards. A team that tries to test the whole backlog will test none of it, and the cards on the riskiest items give the best return.

AI user research comes in two forms. The first analyzes data you already hold: clustering support tickets and summarizing interview transcripts. It works on real evidence, so its main risk is a sloppy summary.

The second form interviews simulated people. AI user research of this kind builds a panel of modeled personas matched to your target profile, asks them your questions, and reports the themes. It needs no recruiting and returns results quickly, which fits a two-day window. Its output comes from a model of users and not from users, and that distinction should appear in every planning document that cites it.

A simulated audience helps most when you want to sharpen a question, and least when you need proof. Nielsen Norman Group's 2024 review of synthetic users found them useful for generating hypotheses and preparing interview guides. It warned against using them for final decisions or concept validation, and for niche populations, because AI tends to please and "every idea is often seen as a good one."

Spend Tuesday collecting answers and Wednesday reading them skeptically. On Tuesday, put each card's question to the modeled audience in neutral wording, and ask what would make them not use the feature. A question phrased as "Would saved filters help you?" invites a yes, while "Walk me through setting up your weekly report" does not.

On Wednesday, compare the answers with what you already know from support tickets and analytics. Mark each assumption as supported, contradicted, or unclear. Treat a unanimous answer with suspicion, because real audiences rarely agree on anything. Also be wary of lists in which the modeled users "seem to care about everything," which is how Nielsen Norman Group describes their weak prioritization.

Whatever stays unclear or high risk goes to real people. Jakob Nielsen's classic analysis found that five users find about 85% of usability problems, assuming comparable users, and he recommended several small rounds over one large one. An AI check is a good way to decide what those five people should be asked.

Each mark maps to a different decision. A contradicted assumption means the ticket is cut or reduced to its smallest useful version. A supported assumption that rests only on simulated evidence is not yet safe, so schedule a real-user check as the first task in the sprint, ahead of the build work that depends on it. An unclear assumption should not receive full scope.

The cost of skipping this step is real. A 2019 analysis of 615 product subscriptions by a product analytics vendor found that about 12% of features generate 80% of daily usage volume, and 80% of features are rarely or never used. That is one vendor's customer base, and the figures cover features that customers chose to track, so treat them as a rough guide. They still suggest that most of a backlog is a bet.

AI user research cannot replace talking to real users. Nielsen Norman Group found that synthetic users gave overly optimistic accounts of their own behavior, such as claiming to finish every course in a study, and that they cannot supply real past experience. It also called presenting synthetic findings as real-user findings "an unethical way to share what you've learned."

Two habits keep a team honest. Label every piece of evidence in the planning document as modeled, observed, or reported, and require that any feature built on modeled evidence has a real-user check scheduled. The review also warned that teams starting with synthetic research may get comfortable and skip the real kind, so put the real check on the calendar before the first AI result arrives.

Can AI user research replace interviews with real users? No. It is a cheap first pass that helps you write better questions and spot weak assumptions. Anything that drives a significant build still needs real people.

How many assumptions should a team test before planning? Three to five. Pick the ones where a wrong answer would cut or reshape a ticket.

How do I stop simulated users from just agreeing with me? Use neutral, open questions, ask what would make them avoid the feature, and treat unanimous answers as a warning sign.

What should I do with results I cannot verify? Mark them as modeled, give them less weight than observed evidence, and schedule a real-user check before committing full scope.

Sources: Scrum Guide, Sprint Planning; Jakob Nielsen, "Why You Only Need to Test with 5 Users" (March 18, 2000); Nielsen Norman Group, "Synthetic Users: If, When, and How to Use AI-Generated 'Research'" (June 21, 2024); 2019 Feature Adoption Report (615 product subscriptions, vendor-reported).

Get the freshest news and resources for developers, designers and digital creators in your inbox each week

AI gets the petabytes, while PCs get the scraps.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The latest CPU-Z hardware survey data shows 32GB and 64GB memory configurations down 2.5% and 18% year on year, respectively. The driving factor is no mystery: High-capacity memory has become a luxury in modern PC builds. The AI rush is still gobbling up the global DRAM supply, so prices for the best RAM options across DDR4 and DDR5 have gone through the roof.

If you are not familiar with CPU-Z, it is a free program that gives you a detailed breakdown of the hardware inside your system. It only tracks systems where it is actually installed and running, so it does not report monthly retail sales figures. Do not take the data as sales figures in any sense. They represent CPU-Z's active user base.

32GB and 16GB are common configurations that have grown over the years. According to the gathered data, 32GB configurations lead CPU-Z's user base with a 37.2% share, with 16GB configurations close behind at 33%. Not a surprise, really, since applications and games have grown more memory-intensive. 16GB has been the baseline for a long time now, and 32GB is now the sweet spot for many people, even gamers.

On the flip side, 8GB configurations have been steadily declining. They make up only 11% of the surveyed systems. 8GB systems are rare nowadays. You may still find them in legacy systems, basic office machines, or extremely budget-limited builds where performance takes a backseat. Meanwhile, higher-end configurations, including 64GB and 24GB, are still in the minority at 7.3% and 2.3%, respectively.

Fresh stats from CPU-Z! 🔎 For the first time ever, RAM capacity on PCs has declined year-over-year : -2.5% for 32 GB and -18% for 64 GB! https://t.co/ya0soA55bRNovember 6, 2026

For the first time, the CPU-Z developer recorded a noticeable decline in higher-capacity configurations. Specifically, 32GB configurations fell 2.5% year over year, while 64GB options dropped a significant 18%. This is likely due to the steep rise in memory prices, which has forced consumers to reconsider the cost-benefit of larger capacities.

As the price per gigabyte goes up, buyers are opting for more affordable capacities. Lower-capacity memory kits have seen a somewhat revival. The 16GB configuration, often considered the minimum, has rebounded by 12.9%. Similarly, 8GB configurations, which were previously in sharp decline, grew 5.5%.

Interestingly, 24GB configurations also increased by 19.7% and have become a compromise for consumers who need more than 16GB of memory but do not want to spend a lot of money on a premium 32GB memory kit.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The current distribution between DDR4 and DDR5 memory types reveals that DDR4 remains the dominant memory standard at 54.5%. In contrast, DDR5, despite being the latest memory technology, is still in its early adoption phase at 32.9%. DDR4 has been the de facto standard for almost a decade now, so it will take time for DDR5 to overtake it.

Nonetheless, the noticeable 6% growth in DDR4 and 2.9% decline in DDR5 suggests budget-conscious consumers are sticking with platforms that still embrace DDR4, such as AMD's Zen 3-powered Ryzen 5000 (codenamed Vermeer) or Intel's 13th Generation Raptor Lake processors. CPU-Z's processor charts show a significant increase in Zen 3 chips, including the Ryzen 7 5700X, Ryzen 5 5600, and Ryzen 5 5500, with figures up to 42.8%, 36.1%, and 67.3%, respectively.

Although both DDR4 and DDR5 have increased in price, the former remains slightly more affordable. Companies are supporting the effort too, such as AMD rereleasing the Ryzen 7 5800X3D, vendors restarting DDR4 and DDR4 motherboard production, or Intel preparing rewarmed Raptor Lake Next chips for 2027.

Google Home is one of the most user-friendly smart home ecosystems, and Google has built out an extensive collection of cameras, speakers, and displays. That positions them as great gifts to expand someone’s smart home.

It’s pretty common to get one or two smart home devices and call it a day, but ecosystems like Google Home begin to come alive when there are several devices working in harmony with each other. For instance, you can start out with the Google Nest Learning Thermostat (4th gen), as many do. It makes A/C and heat controls easy, and even saves you money along the way.

But adding something like the Google Home Speaker with Gemini built-in means you get even more control of your home’s indoor environment. You could go a step further and add a Google Nest Cam (indoor or outdoor) or a Google Nest Doorbell, whether wired or battery. It’s a bit pricier, but I love the Google Nest Cam with Floodlight as a smart home gift option for someone, since it provides more utility.

In any case, adding a couple more foundational pieces to your smart home makes it more useful. And as Google adds features with Gemini for Home, users get access to smart alerts and notifications that those cameras didn’t provide a year or two ago, all within the Google Home app.

If you’re gifting for someone who doesn’t have the beginnings of a smart home setup, Google’s Nest products are a good start. You could start with the Google Home Speaker, depending on their needs. Right now, the Nest Learning Thermostat (4th) gen is down to $219 from $279 at Best Buy – $60 off. That’s the best price we’ve seen from the store, and it’s a good deal overall.

Of course, you can’t go wrong with the Nest Doorbell cameras (wired or battery). Go for the battery edition if you’re limited on wiring options or want to use it at an apartment. Wired is the better choice for homes, since it makes 24/7 recording possible for more security.

#10504. Two Chinese companies cleared to buy banned Nvidia AI chips have empty offices and unknown addresses

Is the U.S. Department of Commerce simply rubber stamping these applications?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

An investigation has found that addresses for two Chinese companies approved to purchase Nvidia H200 chips led to empty offices, and the receptionists at the buildings were unfamiliar with them. According to The New York Times, these companies didn’t just have empty offices; their executives are often linked to other firms sanctioned by the United States for working with and providing advanced technology to the Chinese military.

This news has revealed concerns that the U.S. Department of Commerce is simply rubber-stamping GPU export license applications instead of scrutinizing each one for potential links to Beijing. “I have a ton of concerns that neither the Commerce Department or Nvidia is doing the due diligence needed to ensure that the licensing terms are being followed,” former China director for the White House National Security Council Liza Tobin told the publication.

Many of these approved companies include well-known entities like Baidu, BYD, Tesla Shanghai, as well as ZTE, which has previously been blacklisted from the U.S. market, Alibaba, Tencent, and ByteDance. However, the outfit’s investigation also revealed approval for two relatively unknown firms: New Matrix and Maginfra. The offices for both companies were empty shells with some office furniture, and people working nearby said they’d never heard of the company or seen anyone go to the deserted spaces.

The New Matrix website also reportedly did not contain any information about its owners or its history, although public company records reveal that it was founded by Li Bin, who, along with two other primary owners, are connected to Wise Road, a Chinese private equity firm sanctioned by the U.S. for helping Beijing acquire sensitive chip technologies. Furthermore, Mr. Li chairs Tsinghua Unigroup, a tech conglomerate partly owned by the Chinese government, and owns New H3C Semiconductor Technologies, which has also been added to the Commerce Department’s Entity List for cooperating with the Chinese military.

Maginfra, on the other hand, is suspected of being linked to Inspur, which Washington has blacklisted since 2023 for supporting the People’s Liberation Army, with both companies sharing personnel, patents, and a similar government ownership structure. These two firms aren’t listed as the final users of Nvidia’s AI chips, though. Instead, they’re supposed to assemble them into AI servers, which would then be forwarded to a final user, although it’s unclear who their customers are.

Aside from these questionable non-entities, another company that received a license to purchase H200 GPUs has some suspicious links. Zhejiang Yindun Cloud Technology’s executives are reportedly related to the founder of Range Intelligent Computing Technology Group, a data center operator that has sold compute capacity to Chinese military contractors. It’s also reportedly partly operating out of a Range data center.

President Donald Trump banned the export of Nvidia AI GPUs in April 2025, even those customized to meet the performance ceiling Washington set for AI processors. The president reversed the decision late last year in exchange for a 25% cut of the sale, but it still took more than eight months for the first H200 shipments to arrive in China. Beijing’s refusal to give Chinese tech companies the green light to buy the chips for the first few months of the year caused the delay. But once China lifted its import ban on Nvidia chips, several Chinese companies applied for licenses to buy them, and The New York Times reported that around two dozen firms have already received approval, including these questionable ones.

#10501. AI-Powered Pentesting for DevSecOps: 7 Checks Developers Should Make First

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Security testing used to happen once or twice a year, usually after a release had already shipped. That rhythm does not fit teams that deploy daily. AI-assisted tools now promise testing that runs continuously, chains steps together on its own, and writes up what it found.

For developers, the interesting question is not whether these tools are impressive. It is what has to be true in your pipeline before you let an automated attacker loose on your own systems. This article covers seven checks to make first, each with a pass condition, and one short code example you can run. Some checks, such as written authorization and cost tracking, usually belong to a security lead or manager. For those, the developer's job is to confirm they exist, not to own them.

Testing systems you do not have permission to test is not a gray area. Make sure someone with authority has approved the targets and the time window, and keep that approval with your records. Run the first tests against staging with test data. Production comes later, once you know how the tool behaves and what load it creates.

Pass: The approval is on file, and the SOC knows the window and source IPs.

An automated tester should never decide for itself what it is allowed to touch. Define scope in a file that your pipeline can check: which hosts are in bounds, when testing may run, and whether anything destructive is allowed.

SitePoint's article on security patterns for autonomous agents uses an open-source AI penetration-testing agent as its case study. It covers sandboxed execution, tool permissions scoped to each role, approval gates for high-risk actions, and audit logging. Scope enforcement belongs in that same family of controls. Here is a minimal check that refuses any target outside the allowlist or time window:

Call this before every scan job, and log the result. A tool that is out of bounds should fail loudly instead of quietly continuing.

Pass: An out-of-scope target fails the job, and egress outside the allowlist is blocked.

Findings are only useful if they reach the people who can fix them. Most teams already have a place where security results show up in pull requests or a security tab. SitePoint's walkthrough on automating DevSecOps static analysis with GitHub Actions shows how results written in the SARIF format appear as inline annotations and can block a merge when severity crosses a threshold. If your pentest tool can export SARIF or a similar format, send its findings to the same place.

Pass: The tool exports SARIF or similar, and results show up in PRs or the security tab.

An AI tool can produce a long list of findings. To judge whether they are complete and sensible, you need a reference for what good web security testing covers. The OWASP Web Security Testing Guide is a free, widely used checklist of test categories for web applications. It covers web applications only, so APIs and network services exposed to the internet need their own coverage. Map the tool's findings against it, and note which categories it never touched.

Pass: You know which OWASP WSTG categories you expect the tool to cover.

Vendors describe AI pentesting in similar terms: agents plan attack steps, try them, and verify what works. For a view of how a security vendor explains the approach, CyCognito's overview of AI penetration testing walks through a workflow from scoping to autonomous testing, validation and reporting, and notes that human review stays part of the process. All vendor claims need testing in a demo against your own systems before you rely on them.

Ask the same questions of any tool you evaluate: What does it test beyond known checks? How does it confirm a finding is real? What does it send to external services?

Pass: The vendor has clear answers, including where the target list comes from.

Automated findings need review. A person should confirm that a reported path is exploitable, judge its business impact, and decide the fix order. Make sure this is set up before the first run. Without it, a team can end up with a long backlog of unverified findings and little trust in the tool.

Pass: There is a named reviewer and time set aside each cycle.

Running agents continuously can add up, especially when they call large models. Make sure you can track what each run costs and how many real issues it finds, so you can compare it with other approaches. You also need to be able to rerun the specific test that found a problem after a fix ships and keep the result. A finding is only closed when the retest passes.

Pass: Both are possible before you commit to running the tool continuously.

Does AI pentesting replace manual penetration testing?

Not entirely. Automated testing can run more often and cover more ground, but people are still needed for scoping, complex attack paths and judging impact.

In staging, with test data, inside a written scope and time window.

What should I do with findings from the tool?

Send them to the same place as your other security results, have the named reviewer check them, and retest after fixes.

AI-assisted pentesting can give development teams faster and more frequent security feedback, but only when it runs inside clear limits. Before the first run, check that targets and the window are approved, scope is enforced by your pipeline, findings will land where developers work, you have a baseline to judge the output, you know what the tool tests and sends out, someone owns the review, and you can measure cost and retest. The scope check above is small, but it makes sure the tool stays inside the lines you set.

#10499. Meet the 20 first-place apps from RevenueCat’s Shipaton 2026

A cozy habit tracker, a smarter snippet library, and a rooster that wants you out of bed: this year’s Shipaton winners offer plenty of new apps to explore.

RevenueCat’s annual app-building competition drew 3,985 project submissions in 2026, according to its winner announcement. Across the 20 first-place categories, developers tackled everyday frustrations, built inventive games, and found new ways to connect people.

Here’s a look at every first-place winner:

Read RevenueCat’s full winner announcement

Keeping a routine is easier when there’s something to look forward to. Mio pairs self-care and habit tracking with a pixel companion that grows as you make progress. Check off habits, earn coins, and unlock new companions and collectibles, with friends and groups adding a social reason to keep going.

Mio took Shipaton’s Grand Prize, with RevenueCat highlighting its revenue and growth in a crowded habit-tracking market.

That dog you met on your morning walk can become part of your collection. Boop identifies cats and dogs and turns their photos into stickers for a digital book, giving pet lovers a playful reason to pay attention to the animals around them.

Creator Taiwo Omisore’s “100 Boops” challenge took the app into the real world, where printed pet portraits and conversations with owners helped shape the product. That hands-on approach earned Boop first place in #BuildInPublic.

If you have fond memories of futuristic arcade racers, Nitelore has a familiar appeal: hovering vehicles, neon tracks, and plenty of speed. Chase your own ghost in time trials, take on combat races, or compete online.

Free tracks offer a starting point, while paid unlocks and daily and weekly challenges give players more to explore. RevenueCat’s Best Game judges singled out the art direction, effects, and haptics.

Explaining how you see color can be surprisingly difficult. See as Me: Colorblind makes that conversation visual, using a split-screen camera to compare different types and degrees of color blindness. A shareable link lets someone else see the comparison without installing the app.

Developer Javier Jiménez drew on his own experience with protanopia. That personal perspective helped the app earn the RevenueCat Peace Prize.

Clucky’s rooster is determined to get you out of bed. Turning off an alarm can require solving a problem, moving around, or finding an object, bringing a little friction to the temptation to snooze.

Its personality runs through the entire interface, including a time picker that uses the rooster’s scrolling eyes and yellow beak. That consistent character, animation, and attention to detail earned Clucky the RevenueCat Design Award.

Adversary starts with a familiar challenge: flip cards and find matching pairs. The catch is a hidden Danger card with no matching partner. Reveal it, and an ad becomes part of the board you’re trying to remember.

That idea earned Niv Ben-porath’s game the Catvertising Award. Players can continue with ads after an initial ad-free period, or choose a permanent unlock that removes ads and adds difficulty options.

Put a few phones on the table, and Bubble Games turns their screens into a shared game board. Objects move between devices as friends compete in minigames, including a virtual hot potato.

Built by four software engineering students at Université Laval, the Next Gen Award winner uses the phones people already have to create a shared activity. Its Shipaton submission includes ten minigames. The winner announcement links to the Android release on Google Play.

GRIDLOCK takes a game everyone knows and adds up to four players, online rooms, and eleven special moves. The result offers more ways to disrupt an opponent’s plans than the familiar three-by-three board.

Its HAMM Award win recognizes how the business model fits the game: a shared Credits currency connects rewarded ads, optional packs, and a subscription, while core modes remain free. Players can choose the option that fits how often they play.

Just Add begins with a craving. Type what you feel like eating, describe it aloud, or take a photo, and the app suggests three meals built around it, adding protein, fiber, and healthy fats to round out the plate.

Shopping support, hands-free cooking, and remembered preferences help carry the idea through to dinner. The winner of Abbey’s Kitchen’s nutrition challenge also impressed judges with a dinner-host feature that accounts for other people’s preferences.

The reply you send every week, the PDF you keep hunting for, and the image you paste repeatedly can all live in SnipStash. It organizes reusable text, images, and files across iPhone, iPad, and Mac, with a keyboard, Mac quick palette, hotkeys, and Shortcuts bringing them within reach.

Private iCloud sync connects the library across devices. Its speed and native interface helped it win Christopher Lawley’s productivity challenge.

Knowing what you want to say and saying it aloud are different things. Rehearse – Hard Conversations gives you an AI counterpart to practice with before a difficult workplace discussion.

Choose from 33 prepared scenarios or bring your own situation, then review feedback on clarity, empathy, assertiveness, and structure. A prep card helps you take the useful points into the real conversation. The app won Leadership Heather’s career-coaching challenge for its focused, straightforward approach.

You see a game in a short video, mean to look it up later, and forget its name. Game Magnet lets you share the clip to identify the games and save them to your backlog.

Processing happens in the background, with a notification when the result is ready. Shelves, ratings, and ranked lists help organize what comes next. The app won Mr Lewis Blogs Gaming’s challenge by making discovery and keeping track of games easier.

FeelGood: Movement Menu starts with a short check-in about your energy, available time, and how you want to feel. It then offers a small menu of movement, with an appetizer, main, and optional sides.

The options span yoga, Pilates, strength, walking, stretching, and breathing. Rest is part of the experience, too. Yusra Salam and Ameena Malik’s app won Simone Sharice’s yoga and fitness challenge, with judges praising its calm, inviting design.

BoatBrawl brings arcade boat battles to a group of up to eight players. Steer, fire, and scramble through interactive arenas, with bots and shared-screen play offering more ways to join in.

Rehaan Cubes and Ashwin K M used Kotlin Multiplatform and Compose Multiplatform to bring the game across phones and computers, including iOS, Android, Mac, Windows, and the web. Its smooth browser play helped it take JetBrains’ Ship Kotlin Everywhere award.

Trazo: Golf Ball Tracer adds a ball-flight line to your own golf videos. Select the ball twice to start, and the processing runs on the phone.

Julian Praest’s app won Most Viral App for the way its team introduced the product through organic content. Golf humor and practical advice set up the tracer reveal, making the finished video part of the pitch. For golfers, the appeal is simple: make the shot easier to follow when you share it.

One Piece Card Game collectors know that similar-looking cards can be very different finds. Grandfolio helps identify artwork variants, manage duplicates, and track collection prices and progress.The Best App for Galaxy winner impressed judges with layouts that adapt to folded, tabletop, and open-screen use. There’s an App Store release for Apple users, too, making this a relevant discovery even if your collection starts on an iPhone.

Let’s Get Support by 3Dadz is designed for partners, parents, and friends affected by someone else’s addiction. Check-ins, journaling, boundary-setting tools, and local resources give those supporters a place to focus on their own needs.

Joshua Burgess and Aaryn B. built the app from community conversations in British Columbia, using Replit to turn the idea into a live product. It won Replit’s Idea to Income award. The announcement links to its Android release.

PoquitoTalk: Bocas del Toro turns English into local Spanish voice notes for WhatsApp and helps translate the replies. The recipient doesn’t need to install the app, keeping the conversation in a tool people already use.

Creator Dorien Van den Abbeele drew on years of life in Bocas del Toro. Its Growth Loop Award recognizes extensive growth experiments and an honest account of what the measurements could show. The announcement links to Google Play.

Parky! combines wait times, dining availability, and memories from Disney and Universal visits. Built by former Disney Cast Member Chase Van Derrick, it aims to offer useful help throughout a park day.

Dining alerts can become conversations on the Lock Screen, where you can ask about another time or restaurant. Alongside queue Live Activities and recap messages, those interactive notifications helped Parky! win the Keep Them Coming Back Award.

In Your Own Words offers short, real-world speaking scenarios and feedback on pace, hesitation, and filler words. Speech analysis takes place on the phone, giving users a way to practice the words before they need them.

Natasha Wilson’s app won the Funnel Vision Award for connecting a web quiz, a relevant practice deck, and a web purchase that unlocks access in the app. The introduction to the product begins before the download.

Friendpire turns the idea of a board-game night with friends into a shared hex-tile world. Gather resources, build farms and docks, and grow an empire together. RevenueCat employee Erik MacInnis built the game to make it easier to stay connected with friends who rarely find time to play.

Friendpire won the separate Conflict of Interest Award, which was open to RevenueCat and sponsor employees and judged by Shipaton participants. It is included here as an optional sidebar and is not part of the 20 first-place count above.

From a useful tool for the workday to a game for your next night in, Shipaton’s winners show how much can come from taking an idea all the way to a shipped app. Explore the winners above, or browse RevenueCat’s full announcement for more apps from the competition.

The people were protesting against the use of fossil fuel generators to power data centers.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Amsterdam police have arrested 150 to 200 protesters at a Microsoft data center site on suspicion of trespassing. According to a Facebook post by the authorities [machine translated], tools designed for breaking into the site were seized from the protesters, who were then allowed on their way to protest peacefully. However, it appears that some members of the group decided to enter the construction site “with violence” anyway, forcing the police to intervene and leading to their arrest. There were also some allegedly stolen items from the construction site found among the belongings of some of the protesters, plus damage and vandalism done to the data center site.

This protest is a continuation of the action that started last Friday, where protesters breached the Microsoft data center, which was being owned by Pure Data Centers. It is also the first of many organized by the “Stop Dirty Data Centers” coalition across Europe, with actions planned for Amsterdam, Dublin, Warsaw, Denmark, and several other cities across the continent. The Dutch publication NOS [machine translated] identified the exact group at the site today as Fossielvrij, who are against the use of fossil fuels in data centers such as the jet engines that Elon Musk installed at xAI’s Mississippi data center.

“Gas prices are going through the roof, and new houses are being postponed because of the overcrowded power grid. Yet tech billionaires like Elon Musk and Jeff Bezos are building energy-guzzling, hideous data centers,” the group said on its website. “We can take back the power of Big Tech by pressing the pause button now. Give us the opportunity to democratically decide what we do and do not use AI for, so that it is not at the expense of our living environment and everything that makes our lives beautiful. Then we look at how many more data centers are needed.”

The protest comes amid the AI data center build-out, which has been facing increased opposition across the United States. A Google data center site in Finland is also in trouble after it cleared out around 1,300 acres worth of forest and trees without an environmental impact assessment, displacing wildlife, and leading to a criminal probe on Tuike Finland Oy, the Google subsidiary in charge of the site.

Fossielvrij’s action was supposed to be peaceful, but it appears that another group joined the demonstration and broke through the fences around the site, leading to the police confrontation. The suspects were arrested for domestic violence and then taken to a cell complex, where it will be decided if they will be detained longer. The police also said that “All suspects will be heard afterward.”

#10492. Open-source GPU task manager reveals exactly what's eating your VRAM
