Open source tool is available for Windows, Linux, and macOS.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Microsoft VP Scott Hanselman has revealed a new open-source GitHub project called gputaskman. The name neatly encapsulates the headline role of this tool, a GPU-focused task manager, but its scope might be broader than you expect. This new tool for Windows, Linux, and macOS can show per-process GPU usage, VRAM consumption, active runtimes, and many more stats. It also breaks out AI/LLM task-specific information.

There's a lot going on in my GPU. I'm making a GPU Taskman to get insights to where my GPU memory is going. PRs welcome https://t.co/6FwKqnFWDWNovember 3, 2026

On social media, Hanselman snappily makes clear the technical itch that gputaskman was designed to scratch. “There's a lot going on in my GPU,” wrote the prominent public face of Microsoft's developer ecosystem. “I'm making a GPU Taskman to get insights to where my GPU memory is going.”

There are several example screenshots of GPU Taskman in use by the Microsoft VP. They clearly show the kinds of data that are presented in the terminal-like display that pops up in the tool. Hanselman’s tweet shows that the most VRAM-hungry app on his RTX 4080S system is the desktop windows manager. That is selected in the list, and more details are shown for this task at the bottom of the window. Also, we see AI/LLM apps consuming VRAM, and a few Windows staples.

In a GitHub-embedded image, we see what details are broken out when the llama-server.exe app is selected in the list. The bottom of the window breakdown reveals that Qwen3-0.6B is being used, and there’s a breakdown of its weights, the KV cache, the number of parameters, and more. Very informative.

As the Microsoft developer has made portable Windows downloads of the latest v0.3.0 release of gputaskman, I couldn’t resist giving it a spin. Upon launch, I found that no tasks were using the discrete GPU in my laptop… so I fired up ye olde Unigine Valley GPU benchmark to stir the RTX 4060 from its slumber. I note the live-streaming charts showing GPU load, VRAM use, temperature, and so on are more prominent in my screenshot than in Hanselman’s examples. Perhaps he screen-shotted a previous version of the tool.

There’s a lot more information about gputaskman, its inner workings, and even its source code, on the linked GitHub repository. Hanselman is sharing his “experimental software” tool under the permissive MIT license. Finally, in social media comments, he hints that expanded GPU monitoring capabilities could be coming to Windows as standard.

#10491. Retrying a send is harder than sending

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every integration that moves something out of your system has the same weak point, and it is

not the happy path. It is the second attempt.

Your code posts a document to a provider. The connection drops before the response arrives. You now hold no job id, no confirmation and no idea whether anything left the building. The timeout told you about your network. It told you nothing about theirs.

So you retry, and you either send the thing twice or you never send it at all. Pick one, because

without help from the API you cannot have both.

The first instinct is a client-side guard. Hash the payload, keep a table of what you have already

That works until the duplicate attempt comes from a different process, a different container, or a

queue worker that started after a deploy. Your table is local state defending against a distributed

The second instinct is to ask the provider whether the job exists. That means listing recent jobs

and matching on something, usually the recipient and a timestamp window. It is a search, and

searches have false positives. Two legitimate sends to the same recipient a minute apart look exactly like one send and one retry.

The third instinct is to make the operation naturally idempotent. That works beautifully for a database upsert and fails completely for anything that leaves your control. A document that has been transmitted cannot be un-transmitted by a second call.

An idempotency key, generated by the caller, attached to the request, and honoured by the

The contract is simple. The caller invents a unique string per logical operation. Every attempt

carries the same string. The first request to arrive does the work and records the result against that key. Every later request carrying that string gets the recorded result back instead of doing the work again.

The important part is where the state lives. It is on the server, which is the only place that knows

whether the job exists. Your retry loop stops needing to be clever. It just needs to reuse the key.

Stripe popularised this pattern and the reason it spread is that the alternative is unbounded.

Without it, every caller writes their own deduplication, badly, and the failure only shows up under load.

Document and messaging APIs are where it matters most, because the side effect is visible to

someone outside your company. Fax for Agents is a current example of the pattern taken

seriously. It requires an Idempotency-Key on every mutating call, so a retry hands back the

same job instead of creating a second one.

Idempotency solves the duplicate. It does not solve the other half, which is that you still do not know what happened.

Most integrations model this as a boolean. Sent, or not sent. That is wrong, and you discover it

the first time a transmission half completes.

A useful state machine for a send has more room in it. Fax for Agents publishes six states:

queued, active, partial, failed, unknown and delivered. Two of those are the interesting ones.

unknown means the provider has not said. Not failed, not delivered. The correct response to

unknown is to wait. A retry loop that treats unknown as failure needs rewriting, because that is

how you produce duplicates even with idempotency in place.

partial means some of it arrived. Resending the whole document would deliver the first pages

twice, so partial is a state that must never be retransmitted automatically. It needs a person, or a

And delivered should be the narrowest word in the vocabulary. In that API it is written only when

the provider's evidence for the attempt supports it, never when the request was merely

accepted. The gap between "we accepted your job" and "it arrived" is where most false

Four questions, answerable from the documentation before you write a line of code.

Does it accept a caller-supplied idempotency string on every mutating call, and for how long

How many terminal states does a job have? If the answer is two, the API is hiding something

Is there a state meaning "we do not know yet", and does the documentation say what you should do when you see it?

When a webhook arrives, can you verify it came from them? Signed delivery with an event id is

the difference between a webhook handler and an open endpoint.

None of that is exotic. It is the difference between an integration that degrades and one that

#10490. Google’s AI search fumbles leave schools in the lurch

Good morning, and happy Ocarina of Time day to all who celebrate. Oh, and speaking of celebrating: congratulations to the real winners of the midterms, YouTube and Twitch streamers.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

It’s hard to overstate how reliant US school districts are on Google. Almost every school board in the country purchases Chromebooks for their students, many of whom are able to take them home and treat them as personal laptops. IT and procurement managers like them because they’re relatively cheap and offer zero-touch enrollment, which lowers upfront and ongoing maintenance costs. Teachers also like them because Google Classroom is relatively intuitive and ChromeOS is optimized for most other web-based education platforms.

But this reliance on Google’s tech stack comes at a cost: school boards are forced to comply with the company’s capricious terms of service, in particular when it comes to the creeping proliferation of AI across its products.

Things came to a head earlier this week when the New York Times reported that changes to the Google Search experience in schools will require opting into Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and, perhaps most importantly, AI Overviews. The move actually comes after Google was pressured to make student AI usage opt-in. Google’s justification: search and AI are now “deeply integrated” in the tech stack, so disabling one means disabling the other.

Google’s alternative to AI Overviews is a new Guided Learning feature, which replaces search’s primary function — solving a problem — with a step-by-step guide that helps a student understand said problem.

Former FTC Chair Lina Khan took to X to decry the move: “Google is using its search monopoly to coerce schools into adopting AI,” she said. “This highlights the danger of allowing an unregulated monopolist to control an essential service. Multiple courts have found Google liable for violating antitrust laws, and this latest move shows it remains unchastened. As the public increasingly pushes back against the use of AI in schools, Google is making clear it will punish those who don’t fall in line.”

I have a feeling Google will need to walk back this decision in some form. As 9to5’s Ben Schoon points out, it’s comically easy to disable AI Overviews in the current version of Google Search, and it doesn’t seem to have a detrimental impact on the subsequent results. But Google is likely worried that if it is forced to carve out versions of the search experience for specific user bases, revenue from its burgeoning AI Overviews ad business may be impacted.

Even if Google sticks to its proverbial guns, the move exposes a rift between the public, particularly parents, and the tech giants pushing AI on their users. Furthermore, the AI boom has had a direct impact on educators’ ability to purchase new hardware and upgrade old Chromebook fleets, since component prices have increased by as much as 5x over the past year. Some lower-income districts are even recalling Chromebooks from students, reverting to a shared laptop pool to save money.

For a much less complicated relationship with Google Search, try its Ocarina of Time Easter egg. Search for “Zelda” or “Ocarina of Time,” then tap the Master Sword to travel back to a 1998-style results page, complete with Link’s sound effects. It’s a trip down memory lane to a time when, as even Google acknowledges, things were a whole lot simpler.

Was AI on the ballot? According to Ben Thompson at Stratechery, it was in a big way. Democrats and Republicans both rejected data centers, but Democrats want to actually pass some regulation reining in the hyperscalers’ and frontier labs’ ambitions.

First we were short on compute, but that ramped; now we are short on memory, but that will ramp; we were coming close to being short on power, but that will be rectified. The final constraint is political, and that may be the most imposing constraint of all. Not only is it harder to solve, but it actually interferes with solving the other constraints. Many of the theories about AI assume that it becomes a commodity; I think it’s just as likely we are looking at scarcity for much longer than we expected, which will have ripple effects on everything.

Is it possible to pass broad AI regulation that will slow down an industry that has been allowed to run unchecked since Trump’s reelection in 2024? And is it in the best interest of these companies to support it, even if it means a short-term slowdown at a time when the two biggest AI labs, Anthropic and OpenAI, are about to IPO?

Some fascinating developments in the $6.3 billion Binance hack: turns out it was yet another case of social engineering. A Binance engineer was lured into a fake job interview, before which he downloaded malware that let hackers breach his computer and, eventually, others on the network.

After my discussion with Will last week about the death of browsers, it came as no surprise to learn that Brave is being sued by the New York Times for copyright infringement. Brave is accused of scraping paywalled material from sites like nytimes.com and selling access to it through a search API to AI companies, which use it as training data. Yes, it’s difficult to earn money as a browser maker (see Arc for another compelling example), but this is particularly egregious, even for a company familiar with shady business practices.

You can also support us by becoming a 9to5Google Pro member, starting at just $5/month. You get bonus podcast episodes, access to our members-only Discord server, and lots more.

Thanks for reading, and have a great day. I’ll talk to you on Tuesday.

#10489. Nintendo’s designers strove for ‘elegance’ when remaking Ocarina of Time

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Producers Eiji Aonuma and Hidemaro Fujibayashi talk about bringing the first 3D Zelda to a whole new audience.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Producers Eiji Aonuma and Hidemaro Fujibayashi talk about bringing the first 3D Zelda to a whole new audience.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Ocarina of Time remake on the Switch 2 smashes together the past and present. The game retains much of the original N64 experience while updating it with modern graphics and gameplay that make it feel contemporary. But the mingling of time periods also extends to the development team. While Ocarina’s remake was led by producers Eiji Aonuma (a director on the original game who is now the franchise’s steward at Nintendo) and Hidemaro Fujibayashi (best known for directing Breath of the Wild and its sequel), the team also featured a number of younger developers, including some who had never played the original. According to Fujibayashi, those new voices provided some much-needed perspective that shaped the remake.

“What really impressed me was the fact that they really embraced the theme of what not to change from the original,” Fujibayashi tells me ahead of Ocarina of Time’s launch when explaining the influence of the younger team members, who approached the project “very carefully.” He adds that “I feel confident that they weren’t necessarily pursuing something new for the sake of novelty when it might be unneeded and clutter the experience. They’re looking for elegance. Only what is needed, only what was important in the original, and how to carry that forward as their focus. And I think that’s a perspective they’ll be able to bring to future projects as well.”

Much has changed since Ocarina of Time first debuted on the Nintendo 64 in 1998, and Aonuma has had the unique perspective of working on both iterations of the game. For him, the most obvious difference from then to now is the scope of the production. “We used to have maybe just one person designing the backgrounds, and that’s something that you couldn’t do nowadays,” he says. “A lot of the process is not so different compared to the N64 days, but the scale [is different]: There’s a lot more time involved, a lot more people involved, and because of that it can be harder to manage these teams.”

What has also changed is the perspective of players. The original Ocarina of Time was Nintendo’s first stab at bringing The Legend of Zelda into three dimensions, and there are elements that can feel dated today; you can’t control the camera, for instance, or jump whenever you want. This feeling is only exacerbated by the fact that the most recent Zelda games have put a strong emphasis on player freedom. After building your own flying machine in Tears of the Kingdom, going back to the original Ocarina can feel restrictive. The team made a handful of changes, like giving players complete camera control and the ability to jump anywhere, which were designed to provide connective tissue between the remake and its present-day contemporaries.

“When you’re developing a game like this with the free jump, we have to adjust the world to compensate for that freedom of the player,” Aonuma says. “I wasn’t quite sure about doing this in this title, but as soon as I felt it I thought, ‘Okay, this is a really good way to give players another way to approach a goal in the game.’ It adds just a little bit more freedom compared to the original Ocarina of Time. For me, bringing forward a little more freedom in this title is helpful because it carries something forward from Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom. Having that connection to the recent titles I think was really helpful.”

The same could be said for the game’s overhauled graphics, which blend realism and fantasy art into a visual style that feels distinct from other Zelda adventures. Aonuma says the goal was to “bring out details of the world that people weren’t able to see in the original Ocarina of Time.” He adds that this was “something that we wanted to do in the original, but we didn’t have the technical compatibility at the time in terms of hardware output.”

That said, the core gameplay of Ocarina still holds up remarkably well, particularly compared to other early 3D games. Fujibayashi was working at Capcom when the original launched in 1998, but he distinctly remembers anticipating how Nintendo would approach solving many of the problems that plagued early attempts at big 3D worlds. One of the most impactful features in Ocarina is something called Z-targeting, which lets the player lock on to an enemy, making combat much more intuitive. “The solutions are something that still stick with me as a foundation for when I create 3D games,” Fujibayashi says.

For Aonuma, remaking Ocarina of Time was also an opportunity to address some issues he had with the original version of the game. For instance, one of the most infamous parts of the N64 Ocarina was Navi, a little fairy who serves as a guide for Link during his adventures. The problem was, in the process of trying to help the player, she could become overbearing and, in turn, something of a nuisance. While Navi is still ever-present in the remake, her chatter has been toned down quite a bit.

“One of the things that we were thinking about in [the N64] development process is: How do we lead the player to the end of this adventure?” Aonuma recalls. “Can they follow along with the story? Something that we used was the Navi hints, and some of these were added just because we felt in that moment a palpable need for the player to have a little extra information. But it’s something that’s a hard balance to hit. You don’t want to give an answer outright to a player, you want a little bit of an information gap so that they have an opportunity to think for themselves and make that stretch. The more hints you add, the more Navi would talk, and that would change the player experience. [In the remake] we were looking for ways to make all of that more comfortable and more streamlined. And so using in-game text to chronicle your adventures up to that point, so people could remind themselves what they’ve done on the quest so far, helps us get people to the end goal.”

But perhaps the biggest thing to address was the Water Temple, widely regarded as the most challenging dungeon in the original game. Even Fujibayashi tells me that he remembers it being “quite difficult” when he first played it. On the N64, players had to contend with an awkward camera and were forced to constantly open up a menu to use important items. But much of that has been modernized in the remake, making the dungeon less arduous, as players can now better see and navigate the underwater dungeon. “I think when people make comparisons from their memory now to the original version of the Water Temple they’re going to feel quite a difference,” Aonuma explains after I tell him I found the new version of the dungeon much easier. “So this is really nice to hear. As soon as you said that I thought, ‘We did it.’”

Aonuma also felt it was important to keep Ocarina’s more traditional style of Zelda gameplay alive as the franchise celebrates its 40th anniversary this year. Breath of the Wild showed what could happen when you turned Hyrule into a true open world, Echoes of Wisdom took classic top-down gameplay and mixed it with an inventive new style of puzzle, and Ocarina offers something more straightforward. He believes that having all of those very different iterations on Zelda is part of the reason the franchise has had such staying power. “You need different approaches,” he says. And it’s those new developers, many of whom are fresh to Zelda, that he hopes will keep that philosophy going.

“We’ve had opportunities to add to the playbook of Zelda, if you will, and the language of development,” he explains. “You get to see the progression of these ideas across the franchise. And now, working with younger staff on this remake, we’re having the opportunity to bring in additional new ideas. I really look forward to the future and what’s possible coming from the new staff.”

#10486. Motorola Signature 27 goes official with true flagship specs and a $1,599 price tag

Motorola is officially back in the flagship space, with its new Signature 27 phone available for pre-order starting today, but it’ll cost a pretty penny.

Motorola Signature 27 is, in every sense of the word, a true and proper flagship smartphone, with specs that are more or less unmatched in the US and hold up even in international markets.

The full specs on Motorola Signature 27 include:

Add it all up, this is a true flagship like we’ve never really seen from Motorola, especially in the US.

Motorola also touts that Signature 27 is compatible with GrapheneOS, has an “ArcticMesh” vapor chamber cooling system with diamond-infused thermal gel, and reiterates just how bright that display is. The Tandem OLED technology in use is also mentioned to be 34% more energy efficient and have a 121% longer lifespan compared to traditional OLED panels. The speakers are also tuned by Bang & Olufsen, a name we’ve not seen in the Android space since LG was still in the game.

On the software side, Android 17 is available out of the box with the promise of 7 years of OS upgrades and security updates – no promises on how often those will be delivered, though. Motorola is also the first brand outside of Pixel to fully embrace Gemini Intelligence, with Gboard Rambler, “Create My Widget,” app automation, and Sign-To-Text ASL translation all supported on Signature 27.

Battery life should be excellent with a 7,000 mAh cell inside that Motorola says charges at 90W (wired) and uses silicon-carbon battery tech. Wireless charging speeds top out at 50W on Motorola’s charger and it sounds like it maxes out at 15W on other devices based on the company’s communication with us. There appears to be no Qi2 support either, sadly, even though Motorola supports the tech elsewhere in its lineup.

The cameras are not holding back either. The 50MP main shooter uses a Sony Lytia L910 sensor with LOFIC for improved color, brightness, and video, with two other 50MP sensors for the ultrawide and front-facing cameras. There’s also a 200MP periscope telephoto camera that’s only 3x optical quality, but goes all the way to 100x hybrid zoom. Motorola has also equipped the device with a dedicated EVS action sensor that is specifically used to improve clarity and sharpness on fast-moving objects while using an “Action Shot” mode.

Truly, a full flagship across the board, but oh boy it is not cheap.

Motorola Signature 27 is launching in the US for $1,599 on November 19, with pre-orders open today. That’s for the 512GB/12GB variant that’s also the only one coming to the US, with colors including “Coal Smoke” and “Capulet Olive.” There’s no availability coming to Canada at this time, but US buyers can get a free Moto Watch Ultra (the one with Wear OS) with their pre-order from Motorola.com for a “limited time.” That same offer also includes a free year of the “motocare” extended warranty. There’s no word of carrier launches yet, but Motorola says US units are “universally unlocked.”

What do you think of Motorola Signature 27, and would you pay $1,599 for it?

Sony reported Q2 net profits of $2.84 billion, a 44 percent jump from the same period last year.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Sony is enjoying a sharp profit spike in its latest quarterly financials, largely driven by its PlayStation business and the blockbuster success of Spider-Man: Brand New Day. According to the company’s September earnings, Sony’s net profits climbed to 449.60 billion yen (about $2.84 billion) for the three months ended September — a 44 percent increase compared to the 311.4 billion yen ($1.96 billion) it reported for the same period last year.

Spider-Man: Brand New Day was a notable contributor, which released in July and became the third-highest grossing movie in history by earning $2.52 billion at the global box office. That’s a huge boost for Sony Pictures Entertainment, which posted earnings of $581 million for the three months ended September — a 527 percent jump from the $93 million it reported for the same period in 2025.

Sales of Playstation 5 consoles have fallen, with 2.6 million units reported this second quarter compared to the 4 million that were sold in Q2 2025. While the Sony-exclusive Marvel’s Wolverine sold 2.4 million units against a reported budget of $300 million, PlayStation game sales generally have increased for both digital downloads and physical discs. That’s in spite of the week-long player blackout that took place in August to protest Sony’s plan to ditch discs entirely. Looking forward, PlayStation is now looking at its next generation console, which developers are making “solid progress” on according to Sony CFO Lin Tao, and is “progressing as scheduled.”

You can now ‘Rediscover the thrill of unboxing a GeForce legend’ in a Steam game, but an RTX 5080 is recommended for this path-traced tech demo.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

On this day in 2006, Nvidia released the legendary GeForce 8800 GTX and 8800 GTS graphics cards. Nvidia has decided to honor the flagship of the pair with its GeForce 8800 GTX - 20 Years of a Legend interactive demo, now free on Steam. However, the recommended specs to ‘play’ with this semi-interactive path-traced unboxing experience include a wallet-worrying RTX 5080. Or you could just watch the video, embedded below for your convenience.

The downloadable demo, which also requires 32GB of RAM and 30GB of free storage, was created by Dylan Browne, a senior special effects artist who frequently works with Unreal Engine, as in this case. This experience is said to deliver era-appropriate graphics, but requires the most advanced graphics tech available two decades after the 8800 GTX first hit the scene to work its graphical magic.

To run GeForce 8800 GTX - 20 Years of a Legend on a 2026 PC, you will need at least an RTX 4070 for acceptable performance, according to the Steam page. This software is stuffed with the latest graphics tech embraced by the green team. “DLSS 4.5 with Dynamic Multi Frame Generation, Ray Reconstruction, path tracing, and Mega Geometry” are all present, says Nvidia.

There’s a bit more to the interactive demo than looking around and poking a box with your mouse and then prodding it to fit the shiny new GPU into your “era-accurate PC tower.” An extra layer of fun is promised by the provision of hidden Easter eggs in this experience.

However, the demo appears to show Windows Vista on the PC in the gaming den, which launched at retail a few months after the 8800 GTX. Admittedly, this legendary GeForce card and Windows Vista OS would roam the Earth at the same time, with many folks buying the GeForce 8800 GTX and 8800 GTS to shove into their Vista PCs from 2007 onwards. Crysis launched on PCs in late 2007, a year after the G80 architecture cards debuted.

Nvidia launched the GeForce 8800 GTX (128 shader units) and 8800 GTS (96 shader units) at $599 and $449, respectively. These legendary Nvidia GPUs spearheaded the DirectX 10 era, accelerating existing PC titles and delivering next-gen visuals with new DX10 software. You can read more in our reviews from the time, like the 12-page review of the GeForce 8800 GTX and GTS from the launch week. Briefly, our reviewer thought these cards were “amazing” for their current performance and DX10 promise. They were also excited about the G80 architecture for bringing CUDA to consumers and the promise of making the GPU “something much more than a frame rendering device.” Some Luddite gamers may wish that had never happened.

Nvidia driver support for the GeForce 8800 GTX and GTS would run out of road about a decade later. By that time, DirectX 11 hardware and games were mainstream, leaving older graphics hardware designs obsolete when it came to playing the latest AAA PC titles.

#10482. Moto Buds Ultra 27 and Watch Fit 27 help complete the company’s new premium ecosystem

The duo of Motorola accessories will see a North American release in the coming months, featuring Motorola Qira integration.

If you’re planning on upgrading to the extremely ultra-level Motorola Signature 27 flagship phone this year, I have good news for you: you’ll have plenty of time to save up for Motorola’s accompanying accessories after you buy the $1,600 phone. Motorola isn’t announcing a specific release date or price for either the Moto Buds Ultra 27 or Moto Watch Fit 27, but here’s what you can look forward to when they eventually come out.

Starting with the Moto Buds Ultra 27, which Motorola calls its “most feature-packed pair of earbuds yet.” These sport Bose-branded ANC and SoundDesign tuning, plus six high-SNR microphones with two dedicated Voice Pickup Units in what’s now become a conventional wireless earbud design with tips. Motorola says it created the microphones not just for great sound quality, but also for content creation, which means they’re clearly confident in the quality of these mics.

The buds deliver around 8 hours of continuous playback on a single charge, and the included case adds another 37 hours of use. They’ve also got built-in Motorola Qira support, plus AI Live Translate, head gestures, dual connection and audio share (so you can pair them with two devices, or pair two sets of buds with one device), plus a wear detection auto-pause feature.

They also sport IP57 protection, support Google’s Find Hub, and feature 11mm dynamic drivers for crisp audio. Moto Buds Ultra 27 will debut sometime in the near future in two colorways: Pantone Reflecting Pond (dark blue), and Pantone Temple Gray.

The Moto Watch Fit 27 is the antithesis of the recently launched Motorola Watch Ultra, focusing more on daily health than anything else. That means nearly two weeks of battery life on a single charge (instead of a day or two as Wear OS watches have), plus a Polar-powered health engine that’s designed to get your health metrics right the first time.

The square design features a rotating crown and a customizable physical button below the crown, IP68 and 5ATM protection, Bluetooth connection, built-in Motorola Qira support, all in an aluminum frame. A 1.85-inch gOLED display sports ultra-thin 2mm bezels and Gorilla Glass 3 on top. The display supports always-on visibility, and the built-in Qira assistant gives you hands-free command of the watch’s abilities.

As for health metrics, this one supports SPO2 monitoring, stress insights, heart rate powered by Polar, menstrual cycle tracking, auto activity detection, step and distance counter, over 100 tracked workouts, and dual-frequency GPS for accurate tracking. The Polar engine also supports holistic tracking, including sleep, calories, inactivity, and active workouts. Moto Watch Fit 27 launches sometime in the future in three colorways: Pantone Stratified Sea, Pantone Pigeon, and Pantone Ballerina.

#10480. How to Build an AI-Assisted Workflow That Does Not Leak

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A generative AI tool can draft a client email, summarise a forty-message thread or refactor a function in seconds. That speed is real, and it explains how fast these tools have become part of the ordinary working day, used to summarise threads, draft replies and reformat data in a single paste. The same ease is the problem. It moves sensitive material into systems that were never scoped to hold it, quietly and at scale.

Here is the uncomfortable part for anyone running a lean team. The risk in that workflow is almost never the model being wrong. It is that nobody decided what should go into the tool in the first place, and nobody is accountable for where the data ends up. AI made the drafting instant. It did nothing on its own to make delegation safe.

This article is about the control that closes that gap. It is not a product and it is not a policy document. It is a person, placed deliberately in the workflow, making the one call that no model can make for you.

Samsung learned this in public. In 2023, engineers in its semiconductor division were given access to ChatGPT to help debug source code. Within weeks, staff had pasted proprietary code and internal meeting notes into the tool, and Samsung banned generative AI across the company soon after. The story ran everywhere, from Bloomberg to The Register. Nothing malfunctioned. People simply moved sensitive material into a system that was never scoped to hold it, and the material was gone.

Two things make this failure mode easy to hit, and both are structural rather than careless.

First, most of these tools run in the browser. The data loss prevention software a security team relies on was built to watch files, endpoints and network egress, and it often cannot see a paste action into a web app at all. Worse, much of this activity happens through personal accounts that sit outside the controls the organisation does have. The leak does not trip an alarm because nothing was downloaded and nothing was emailed. Text was typed into a box.

Second, consumer-grade accounts can retain what you give them. OpenAI's own documentation states that content from consumer ChatGPT may be used to improve its models unless the user turns that setting off. Paste a client list or a contract clause from the wrong account, and that material may now sit inside a training pipeline you do not control and cannot claw back.

For a developer or a founder, the lesson is not "do not use AI". It is that the point of failure sits one layer above the model, in how the work is handed off and who decided what was safe to hand over.

If any of the data moving through that workflow identifies a person, a client, a candidate, an employee or an investor, then GDPR treats the tools and people touching it as processors acting on your behalf. That sounds abstract until you translate it into design constraints, which is what a technical team can actually work with.

Under Article 28 of the GDPR, anyone who processes personal data for you must do so under a binding contract that sets out what is processed, for what purpose and for how long, and everyone handling that data must be under a confidentiality commitment. An AI vendor you have never papered is a processor you have not accounted for. A free account used quietly by one engineer is a sub-processor nobody signed off. The obligation does not disappear because the tool was convenient.

Location is the second constraint, and it is the one most teams miss. A person, contractor or tool processing your data outside the EU means personal data is being transferred outside the EU, which requires a specific safeguard such as the Standard Contractual Clauses on top of the Article 28 contract. Keep the procesing inside the EU and that entire category of transfer risk falls away before you have to manage it. For UK teams the position is the same: the Information Commissioner's Office sets out the identical core terms for a processor contract, documented instructions, confidentiality, security measures, rules on sub-processors, and deletion or return of data at the end of the engagement.

There is now a second regulatory layer as well. The EU AI Act, which entered into force in 2024, adds obligations on how AI systems are deployed and governed, on top of the data-protection duties GDPR already imposes. The detail is still settling, but the direction is clear: unmanaged, undocumented AI use is getting harder to defend, not easier. None of this is exotic. It is the same governance you would apply to any third party with access to your systems. AI simply introduced a new processor that most workflows never wrote down.

"Human in the loop" usually gets discussed as a quality measure: a person checks the output so errors do not ship. That is true, and it undersells the point badly. The human is also the only part of the workflow that can make a judgment about what should never enter the tool at all.

A model cannot decide that a board deck stays out of a consumer chatbot. A browser extension cannot know that one folder holds client data governed by a contract that forbids sub-processing without written consent. Those are judgment calls, and judgment is precisely the thing you cannot automate. The person doing the delegating is your policy enforcement point, sitting at the exact moment the decision is made. They decide what gets pasted, what gets redacted first, what runs only inside an enterprise account with training switched off, and what a human handles end to end because the risk is simply not worth the minutes saved.

This reframes the division of labour that actually holds up. The AI owns the mechanical layer: summarising a long thread, turning rough notes into a first draft, extracting action items, reformatting data. The human owns the judgment layer: what matters, how it is framed, and, above all, where the data is allowed to go. Remove the human and you have not automated the work. You have removed the only control that was reading the situation and deciding what was safe.

It is worth being specific about what that judgment looks like in practice, because it is a skill and not a job title. Someone exercising it knows which inputs are off limits without having to ask, keeps sensitive material inside controlled systems by default, and treats the question "should this go into the tool at all" as part of the work rather than an afterthought. Whether that person is an engineer, an operations lead or an executive assistant, the control is the same: someone with enough context to know what is sensitive, and enough standing to say no. That habit is what separates a workflow that is genuinely governed from one that merely has a policy document nobody reads.

The practical version, for a founder or a small technical team that wants the speed without the exposure:

Most teams can put the first four in place in about a week. The last two are harder, because they are not configuration changes. They are about whether the workflow still holds when the people in it change, and that is the part most teams discover they got wrong only after someone has already left.

The speed of AI is not in question. What AI cannot supply is the judgment about what should enter it, and the accountability for where the output goes. Treat the human in the loop as a security control, design the workflow around that control, and you keep the nine-second draft without the quiet data leak. Remove the human, and the only thing you have really automated is the leak.

#10478. Discord is ditching Electron and its new app uses less RAM

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Discord is moving its apps fully over to the Rust programming language.

Discord is moving its apps fully over to the Rust programming language.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Discord is finally giving up on Electron, the cross-platform framework that lets developers build desktop apps using web technologies. Electron web apps are notoriously heavy on system resources, and Discord is now moving its desktop app fully to Rust — a modern programming language designed for speed and memory safety.

“We’ve been early adopters of Rust since 2018 and a lot of our infra, voice tech, and even some of the current client is already Rust, the holy grail was getting the whole client there,” explains Discord chief technology officer Stanislav Vishnevskiy in a post on X. “That means Electron goes away, and so does React Native, mobile keeps native UI but shares app code with desktop via Rust.”

Vishnevskiy also shared an image of the Discord Windows client using just 290MB of RAM, which is a clear improvement as you only have to participate in a few servers and RAM usage can easily hit 1GB or more using the current Windows app.

It’s not clear when Discord will make the full switch over to Rust, but Vishnevskiy says the company “hope to get it into testing with users soon.”

Commanders needed a combat robot to operate in underground gas pipelines, tunnels, trenches, and similar environments.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Ukraine is developing a new class of combat robots designed to operate inside underground gas pipelines, where Russian troops have repeatedly attempted to bypass frontline defenses, United24Media reports. The project, disclosed by Ukraine's 2nd National Guard Corps, Khartiia, involves modifying unmanned ground vehicles (UGVs) to perform reconnaissance and combat missions in spaces too narrow for conventional military machinery. Notably, the work is being carried out by specialists from the corps’ Lava Unmanned Systems Regiment, following Operation Veles — an underground assault that saw Ukrainian soldiers advance more than 15 kilometers through pipeline infrastructure near Kupiansk. Khartiia says the operation killed more than 100 enemy soldiers and resulted in over 30 captured, although those battlefield figures have not been independently verified.

The commander of Lavas ground robotic system battalion, identified by the callsign ‘Matematyk’, said the project addresses a category of warfare that requires a different approach from conventional battlefield robotics. “There is a very long list of tasks that need to be accomplished to fight underground and counter the enemy underground. This is a new class of technologies and challenges that we will have to confront,” he said.

The machines are being adapted from existing groundrobots,rather than developed as conventional surface vehicles. They must be compact enough to navigate narrow pipelines while retaining the ability to carry out military assignments . Most commercially available underground machinery, meanwhile, is designed for construction, drilling, and tunneling — not combat.

As of now, Khartiia is already developing several robotic systems to address those limitations, with the broader objective of sending machines into dangerous underground environments where soldiers would otherwise have to conduct reconnaissance, confront enemy positions, clear positions, or clear out themselves. “War is already underground, and it will expand. The question is how we adapt to this war,” Matematyk said.

The project follows unusual battlefield developments near Kupiansk, where Ukrainian forces used the same underground infrastructure that Russian troops have previously exploited to infiltrate behind Ukrainian defensive positions. During Operation Vels, Ukrainian soldiers moved through a gas pipeline measuring approximately 1.2 meters in diameter, according to a report by Ikrainska Pravda. The confined passage made movement and close-range firefights particularly,with Ukrainian troops having little-to-no room to maneuver.

Russia had previously used the pipeline network beneath the Oskil River to move soldiers toward Ukrainian-held territory. The Ukrainian battlefield monitoring group ‘Deepstate' reported an earlier Russian infiltration attempt in September 2025. Last month, President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said that Ukrainian forces had eliminated more than 100 Russian troops and captured over 30 during the operation. The experience also exposed the limitation of sending soldiers into such environments. Underground operations restrict visibility,complicate communications , and leave little room for maneuvering equipment.

That said, Ukraine has increased its reliance on unmanned military systems as battlefield terrain proves complicated to navigate. In July 2024, we reported on Ukrainian forces deploying AI-controlled drones capable of independently identifying and attaching targets. The country has also been experimenting with unmanned systems that operate beyond conventional aerial platforms. In August, the US Army tested an autonomous Ford F-250 breaching vehicle, which builds on the Lancer unmanned ground vehicle program and uses a counter-drone turret to protect military operations from aerial attacks.

However, autonomous ground systems still face considerable technical challenges. Russia's Shturm uncrewed assault tanks , for instance , repeatedly lost their command connection during the Tsentr-2026 military exercise. The vehicle eventually became stuck in mud, even after organizers switched to manual driving. Russia has also tested an AI powered Shahed variant using Nvidia's Jetson Orin computing platform. The system was reportedly capable of identifying targets without relying on externally supplied coordinates.

#10472. Xbox overhauls its PC, web, and cloud apps with a faster design

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Xbox Insiders can start testing the new PC app today.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft is unveiling a new Xbox PC app that launches “up to 60 percent faster” than before, with an overhauled design to match new updates to Xbox Cloud Gaming on the web and smart TVs. Xbox Insiders will be able to start testing the updated Xbox PC app today, and the updated Xbox Cloud Gaming interface will start rolling out over the next few weeks.

Ever since Microsoft launched its Xbox Ally handheld devices last year, it was clear the Xbox PC app still needed a lot of work to feel more like an Xbox console experience on Windows. Microsoft has greatly simplified the app now, with a design that no longer feels like you’re clicking a mouse when you’re using a controller.

“The app now launches up to 60 percent faster,” says Jason Beaumont, VP of Xbox experiences. “Pages like Home, Store, and Game Pass load up to 50 percent faster, and in-app windows open up to 65 percent faster.” The Xbox PC app also has simplified navigation that should make it easier to use on a controller, and the library section now has updated filters and better ways to organize your installed games.

Microsoft has improved the social parts of the Xbox PC app, too, with friends now listed in the main page and quick buttons to start a chat or join a party. The app overall feels less like a web one now, with what appears to be a mix of native and web elements. Microsoft has also refreshed the game card UI, as well as the home and settings sections. Even the animations have been updated to make this feel more like an Xbox dashboard experience.

I’ve been using this new Xbox PC app for the past few weeks, and it’s noticeably faster on a variety of devices I own. I’ve found it’s much easier to navigate around with a controller, and the search interface no longer tries to pop up when you’re moving between sections.

This redesign does make the Game Bar interface on handhelds feel a little out of place now, though. I understand Microsoft is looking to improve that in the coming months, and there should be design updates to make it match more closely to this updated Xbox PC app.

This new UI is also coming to Xbox Cloud Gaming. “We’re also bringing the refreshed Xbox web experience to more players and rolling out updates to Xbox Cloud Gaming apps on supported TVs and VR headsets,” says Beaumont. “These changes are part of our work to make Xbox faster, simpler, and more familiar wherever you play.”

The refreshed web version of Xbox Cloud Gaming has a similar look and feel to the Xbox PC app. A new library tab highlights cloud games across your own library and Xbox Game Pass. A desktop mode makes better use of larger displays, and you can choose between dark and light modes, as well as five new high-contrast options designed to improve accessibility support.

A similar design will also be available on smart TVs and VR headsets soon, but these designs aren’t exactly the same across PC, web, and smart TVs as Microsoft has customized them. “Playing on a PC, TV, or VR headset is different, and each experience needs to work well for the screen you’re using,” says Beaumont. “Wherever you play, the basics should feel familiar: finding your games, knowing what you can play, and getting into a game quickly.”

#10471. The end of an era as Linux 7.5 purges the last 30-year-old Intel MMX optimizations from Pentium age

Get started with free access to reviews, badges and discussions.

Unlock exclusive tools and insights for enthusiasts who want more.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

MMX was introduced in 1997 and marketed as a MultiMedia eXtension for Intel’s processor architecture in shiny new PCs with CD-ROMs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Linux developers are preparing to make Intel’s ancient MultiMedia eXtensions an irrelevance. Intel introduced MMX in the Pentium era, but a patch expected to merge into mainline Linux 7.5 will remove the last remaining optimized software from the open-source OS. Expected to be released in 2027, the new kernel will wipe MMX-optimized code from its software RAID XOR code, explains Phoronix.

Admittedly, there wasn’t much left in Linux in terms of MMX optimizations. But few folks will be running the most modern kernels on Pentium MMX and Pentium II chips in 2026. And most other MMX-optimized code disappeared from the OS half a decade ago, when some MMX-optimized memory management code was removed. Note that, if you are a determined late-90s Pentium devotee, you can still use software RAID, but it will lack the MMX fast path.

This is a classic case of Linux code housekeeping. With this change, the kernel will lose over 800 lines of code and replace them with just 12.

Intel first introduced MMX Pentium chips in January 1997. Though I vaguely remember these processors being marketed as being enhanced by ‘MultiMedia eXtensions,’ Wikipedia insists that “MMX is officially a meaningless initialism[6] trademarked by Intel.”

In a subsequent trademark battle with longtime foe AMD, a document produced in court indicated that MMX stood for ‘Matrix Math Extensions.’ AMD relied on this to sidestep the trademark, as an initialism cannot be trademarked, according to the encyclopedic source.

Eventually, all rival x86 processors managed to integrate MMX instructions – so as not to be pushed into the shade during the multimedia boom. A surprisingly large number of non-Intel chips ended up offering MMX, too. Along with Pentium MMX chips, AMD, Centaur, Cyrix, Rise, and Transmeta eventually offered MMX CPUs.

After MMX's marketing success, AMD pioneered 3DNow! instructions, and about a year later, Intel fired back with SSE and SSE2.

#10469. Sony revives the Walkman’s iconic MDR-3 headphones with USB-C for $60

Sony is bringing back its iconic Walkman MDR-3 headphones for the modern era with a USB-C connection and a price tag of $60.

Originally launched in 1979, Sony’s MDR-3 headphone lineup served as a companion to the Walkman cassette player and, now, they’re making a comeback. The Sony MDR-3R headphones are a USB-C revival of the classic design, just with connectivity that actually works on modern devices.

The design, Sony says, is a “faithful recreation” of the original and comes with blue or orange ear pads. Even the packaging and included manual “inherit the design of the original model.” As for the actual sound quality, Sony has “re-engineered” the same 23mm driver with sound “close to the original,” though refined for modern sound.

Along with the USB-C connection, they also feature a microphone and in-line remote, and Sony incorporated recycled plastic too.

Sony MDR-3R are available starting today for $59.99 from typical retailers.

Celebrating the Walkman’s role in Netflix’s Stranger Things, Sony is also releasing a red/black variant to match the ones worn by Max in the show. Those will cost $79.99.

#10466. AAWireless expands beyond Android Auto adapters with new cooled Qi2 car charger

AAWireless is known for helping you convert a wired Android Auto connection into a wireless one, but the company is now launching a new product – an active-cooled Qi2 car charger.

Update 11/5: After debuting solely on the company’s website, AAWireless Charge is also now available on Amazon in the US.

“AAWireless Charge” is a Qi2 25W wireless car charger that costs $50. The charger is compatible with MagSafe, Pixelsnap, and Qi2 in general, and supports up to 25W charging speeds on compatible phones.

“Charge” also includes active cooling support with a built-in fan that the company says is “quiet” when active.

Included in the box you’ll find both an air vent mount as well as an adhesive base. There are also two magnetic ring adapters in the box for “Qi2 Ready” phones that don’t have magnets built-in or a magnetic case available. AAWireless says to use your car’s 12V outlet with the included adapter for “best performance,” but you can also use existing USB-C connections so long as they deliver enough power.

To be clear, there’s no wireless adapter built into the charger (as cool as that would be), but you can use this alongside an AAWireless adapter while driving, the company specifically mentions.

“AAWireless Charge” is available from the company’s website and is also on Amazon.

#10464. Samsung’s next cheap phone will have a much bigger battery than the Galaxy S26 Ultra

According to a new report, Samsung’s upcoming mid-range smartphone is getting a big battery upgrade that outdoes that company’s current $1,400 flagship.

GalaxyClub reports that the Samsung Galaxy A38 will ship with a 6,000 mAh battery. That’s a 20% jump over Samsung’s typical 5,000 mAh batteries that have been in Galaxy A series phones for the past few years, and is also a 20% jump from the company’s current flagship Galaxy S26 Ultra.

Compared to the likes of many other Android phones on the international market, 6,000 mAh isn’t huge. Honor, for example, launched new phones with 10,000 mAh batteries just a couple of weeks ago. But in the context of Samsung, this is a welcome step forward. The company finally embraced silicon-carbon in its latest foldables and, while it’s unclear if that’s what we’re seeing here, it’s very obvious that Samsung is making a push to increase its battery capacity moving forward.

Based on the Galaxy A37’s launch date, the Galaxy A38 is likely to make its debut in early 2027.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Electronic Arts is launching 25 classic games on Steam, including “Anniversary Editions” of the motorcycle racer Road Rash and six Wing Commander titles. These Anniversary Editions preserve “the original look, feel and creative intent of the game” but also have quality-of-life updates to support modern systems and controls and improve performance, according to EA.

The seven new editions were “inspired” by the discovery of a “physical media vault” with “thousands” of Betacam, VHS, and D1 tapes and “other media assets,” EA says. As part of the digitization process, EA was able to recover master FMV sequences and reedited them to restore more than 1,500 scenes from the games. The updated Wing Commander games also officially incorporate some community-made mods.

You’ll be able to get the Road Rash and Wing Commander Anniversary editions for $9.99, and the other classic games, including Ultima and Crusader titles, will be available for $4.99. You can grab them on Steam, GOG, and the EA App.

#10462. How to Learn Java with AI: A Practical Guide for Beginners

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Java has been one of the most in-demand programming languages for over two decades. It powers Android apps, banking systems, enterprise software, and huge backend platforms. The good news: learning it has never been easier. With AI tools like Claude, ChatGPT, and GitHub Copilot, you now have a patient tutor available at any hour.

The catch is that AI can help you learn faster or help you avoid learning at all. This guide shows you how to use it the right way.

Traditionally, learning Java meant reading a textbook, watching videos, and getting stuck on a compiler error for two hours with nobody to ask. AI removes much of that friction:

Instant explanations. Ask "what is the difference between an abstract class and an interface?" and get an answer pitched at your level.

Personalized pace. Ask for the same concept explained three different ways until it clicks.

Error decoding. Paste a confusing stack trace and get a plain-English explanation of what went wrong.

Unlimited practice. Generate fresh exercises on any topic, any time.

But AI is a tutor, not a replacement for practice. You still have to write the code yourself.

Before writing code, you need the JDK (Java Development Kit) and an IDE such as IntelliJ IDEA, Eclipse, or VS Code.

"I'm a complete beginner on Windows. Walk me through installing the JDK and IntelliJ IDEA, and help me run my first Hello World program."

If something goes wrong (and it often does with PATH variables), paste the exact error message and ask what it means.

Use AI to build understanding with analogies and examples.

"Explain Java loops like I'm a beginner. Give me one real-world analogy, then three short examples that get progressively harder."

Then close the chat and write your own loop from memory. That second step is where learning actually happens.

OOP is where many beginners struggle, and where AI shines as a tutor. Concepts like classes, objects, inheritance, polymorphism, encapsulation, and abstraction are easier to grasp when you can ask follow-up questions.

"Teach me inheritance in Java using a Vehicle, Car, and Bike example. Then quiz me with 5 questions, one at a time, and wait for my answer before giving feedback."

Asking the AI to quiz you, rather than just explain, turns passive reading into active recall.

Once you've written a program, ask AI to review it. This is one of the most valuable habits you can build.

"Review my Java code. Point out bugs, style issues, and anything that isn't idiomatic. Explain why each suggestion matters, but don't rewrite the whole thing for me."

That last sentence is important. You want feedback and understanding, not a finished solution you copy and paste.

When your code throws a NullPointerException or ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException, resist the urge to say "fix this." Instead, try:

"Here's my code and the error. Don't give me the answer yet. Ask me guiding questions so I can find the bug myself."

Debugging is a core professional skill. Using AI as a Socratic coach builds that skill instead of bypassing it.

Tutorials only take you so far. Projects are where Java sticks. Ask AI to suggest projects matched to your level:

Beginner: Number guessing game, to-do list in the console, simple calculator

Intermediate: Library management system, bank account simulator, student record manager

Advanced: REST API with Spring Boot, inventory system with a database, a chat application

"I know Java basics and OOP. Suggest a project I can finish in a weekend, break it into milestones, and don't write the code for me."

Copy-pasting without understanding. If you can't explain a line of code, you haven't learned it. Ask the AI to explain it line by line.

Trusting AI blindly. AI can produce code that looks right but is wrong, outdated, or insecure. Always run it, test it, and verify against official documentation.

Skipping the struggle. A little frustration is productive. Try for 15 to 20 minutes before asking for help.

Never writing code from scratch. Regularly practice without AI so you know what you can actually do on your own, especially before job interviews.

AI is a fantastic assistant, but it can't tell you what you don't know you need to learn. A structured course gives you a proven roadmap, so you cover the right topics in the right order, while AI fills in the gaps, answers your questions, and keeps you moving.

The best way to learn Java with AI is to treat it like a good mentor: ask it to explain, challenge, and review, but do the typing yourself. Learners who combine consistent practice with smart AI use progress faster than ever before.

Operator ALL.Net apologized on Nov 2, but has only just begun to ‘gradually resume operation on a trial basis starting today.’

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The sensory overload typically experienced when visiting video game arcades in Japan was eerily absent from some venues this week, reports ITMedia News [machine translation]. It says that a Sega system outage meant there was “no end in sight” for the rows of silent black screens. However, a recent tweet from arcade network operator ALL.net indicates that both Sega and Bandai Namco machines are affected. Moreover, arcade lovers will be pleased to hear that affected arcades will “gradually resume operation on a trial basis starting today.”

a friend sent me some pics of their local arcade and LMAO the sega outage is nuts https://t.co/3vSYjCYAIZNovember 3, 2026

ALL.Net, a contraction of Amusement Linkage Live Network, was launched by Sega in 2004 to allow arcade games to look up to the net for auto updates, data logging, implement online play, and power national leaderboards. Sega titles like Initial D The Arcade, Maimai, and CHUNITHM, rely on this network, as do Bandai Namco games like Aiko no Tatsujin, Mario Kart Arcade GP DX, and Gundam Extreme Vs.

In its official apology blog post, dated November 2, ALL.Net / Sega Fave said that it suspended services a day prior after it became aware of its servers being affected by “unauthorized access by a third party.” It reassured customers that their personal info and billing data were not on the compromised server. However, it asked for more time to investigate, run safety checks, and so on, before restoring service.

This morning ALL.Net told customers that Bandia Namco titles “will gradually resume operation on a trial basis starting today (online play and card use enabled).” Sega products remain under maintenance, and online services are unavailable. There is currently no estimate for issue resolution, but a news bulletin is promised for November 10 (Tuesday).

Classic offline arcade machines weren’t affected by this outage, of course. Moreover, rival arcade gaming legends like Taito and Konami run their own, separate, arcade networks and authentication systems.

#10457. How Remedy designed Control Resonant’s wildest mission: ‘Anything kind of goes’

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Time-travel stories are ‘illogical’ by nature. But for Resonant’s version to work, Remedy had to make the physical space a lot easier to follow.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Time-travel stories are ‘illogical’ by nature. But for Resonant’s version to work, Remedy had to make the physical space a lot easier to follow.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Note: Some spoilers for Control Resonant’s “Recursions and Iterations” mission follow.

One of Control Resonant’s most memorable missions involves time travel — kind of. There are certainly multiple timelines intersecting, or maybe overlapping, or maybe running in parallel. As protagonist Dylan Faden, you meet some of the researchers who are trapped in and investigating these timeline anomalies. Dylan’s first encounter with the researchers is somehow not the researchers’ first time meeting Dylan. But before we can learn much more, time glitches out, and they all turn up dead.

That seems to be the end result in every timeline Dylan comes across, even toward the end of the mission. What does it all mean for this timeline, the one we’re playing? Did the act of tracking down the researchers change our timeline? Are we in a different timeline entirely?

I wasn’t sure what to make of it when I finished the mission, and I’m not alone — even the developers at Remedy had trouble keeping track of it all. “I don’t actually remember where we landed on with the writing,” says lead level designer Anne-Marie Grönroos, turning the question over to senior level designer Admir Burnic. “Like, are the researchers even there after the mission is done? Are they still living their life?”

Burnic isn’t sure, and I tell them I’m not entirely sure, either. But that’s not really the point of the mission. Like many of Remedy’s best sequences, the “Recursions and Iterations” mission is more about the unease of knowing something is wrong but not exactly what it is, the subtle horror of realizing generally what’s been happening but not necessarily why. Maybe the narrative team did establish a canon ending for the researchers and all three of us just missed it, or maybe it was ambiguous on purpose.

“It’s funny, when you’re dealing with this sort of time-travel stuff, timelines, whatever, there’s always this aspect to it where it’s almost illogical,” Burnic says. “So anything kind of goes. I mean, it could be intentional. It could also not be intentional.”

Toward the end of “Recursions and Iterations,” there’s a mind-bending combat sequence in which a bunch of Dylans, presumably from the other timelines, glitch in to join the fight. Burnic says this was a somewhat late addition inspired by “Effect and Cause,” a mission from Titanfall 2.

There’s this time-travel mission in Titanfall 2. I was really inspired by that. I wanted to do something along those lines, but I was looking for “What’s the Remedy version of it?” And at some point I had this idea of glitches of Dylan showing up executing enemies. I remember it was fairly late in production, so it was a bit risky. I knew I can’t just go and pitch this. I have other stuff to do at the same time. So I was finding something that was relatively easy to do, a way to reuse Dylan’s animations that we already have. So I remember just going to one of our animators and going, “Hey, can you just throw me some of these animations? I have an idea.”

Either way, the storyline was a consistent standout for Remedy’s playtesters, Grönroos and Burnic tell me. What they had to fine-tune — and very intentionally so — was the language and narrative of the physical space. In the final version of the mission, Dylan has to travel between three different research stations in the labyrinthine Underpass, an expanse of floating concrete islands, to search for the researchers. Ambiguity here was going to be a problem.

“In the beginning, it was, like, identical stations, and we would more rely on the narrative content inside of stations to [tell you] what happened there,” Burnic says. In the final version, the stations share the same layout but are in visibly different states of disarray and disrepair. “We found a sort of language we could use. Station A was, ‘They were here for two weeks,’ station B [was], ‘They were here for three months,’ and I think the last one was the extreme one, which was like, ‘Oh, this was 10 years into this.’”

The team used that concept as a guide to visually differentiate each station from the others. “And that came very much from a need,” Grönroos explains. “When we had people playtesting that mission before, they didn’t even know how many stations there were. Like, there were people who thought there’s one station, maybe there’s six stations, who knows, because they look so identical and the environment is not that easy to tell apart, either.”

There were, aptly, a lot of iterations of this, and art was just part of it. One of the biggest changes was mechanical: You need the Reach ability, which allows Dylan to essentially grapple over long distances, to access the Underpass area to begin with. This wasn’t an explicit requirement early on, but it makes the floating islands of the Underpass much easier to navigate. And “it was the navigation issues that were keeping this mission down” during development, Grönroos says.

But Dylan’s abilities presented their own problems. “If I remember correctly, we wanted to have certain beats, certain narrative moments, being represented to you first, and I think one of the biggest difficulties for me especially was that Dylan as a character is so powerful. The gaps he can close with his powers is quite insane, right? And it was so easy to break [the flow],” Burnic says.

“I remember at some point kind of realizing that, however you twist and turn it, you can never really guarantee that a player sees this, and trying to guarantee it will be a bad design because it will be too hand-holdy,” he adds. “It was more like these narrative moments were pieces of the puzzle, almost. So it doesn’t matter which one you get to first, but hopefully the player will be able to put them together once they have all the pieces.”

With a supremely powerful character like Dylan, Burnic says, “you need to lean into it and just design in a way where you can trust the player.” And with multiple timelines involved, seeing certain things out of the intended order wasn’t going to break the overall story of the mission.

I certainly got turned around a few times in the Underpass and doubled back to look for collectibles I might’ve missed. But I didn’t struggle too much, and I understood the story Remedy was trying to tell — maybe not every single detail possible, but the most important beats, and definitely the unsettling, otherworldly mood it created.

That was ultimately the “fragile balance” they had to strike, Grönroos says: trying “to have enough guidance that you understand what you are doing and where you’re supposed to be going” without holding the player’s hand.

“It’s in the DNA of the whole game, really.”
