#10417. Payday Aces High review: This year’s must-play multiplayer VR game

Join up with three other heisters and get revenge on the billionaire who sent you to jail in the first place in Payday Aces High, now available on Meta Quest, SteamVR, and Steam Frame.

Payday Aces High is the return to the series’ roots I had been hoping for. Payday 3 wasn’t received particularly well, and while the team behind it has turned things around in the past few years, it’s pretty clear that Payday 2 is still everyone’s favorite entry. But that stops for me now as we enter a new chapter in the series, with four new characters, a new “villain,” and made-for-VR mechanics that shine.

I’ve been playing Payday Aces High throughout the alpha and beta stages and got access to the final review build a week ago. Since then, my son and I have robbed banks, stolen from museums, hoisted a fragile quantum computer out of an icy compound, and generally made life a living hell for Warren Jupiter, the man who made billions framing the characters you play as in Payday Aces High.

If you’re familiar with Payday games, this one will mostly feel like a return to the original game with new mechanics in tow. Some of the fan-favorite mechanics from Payday 2 (and even 3) aren’t here — you can’t take hostages, for example — but other mechanics, like gun upgrades and perks, will keep you coming back over and over again.

While you can choose from the four main Aces characters — the Ace of Hearts, Clubs, Diamonds, and Spades — the character itself is just a visual change rather than a stats-based one. You customize your own stats throughout the adventure as you unlock points and earn money. You’ll also collect the infamous masks that each character dons, including DLC sets (and missions) scheduled for the next few months.

Payday Aces High begins with six unlockable missions, while a seventh free one (named Coup de Grace) is landing on December 14. Developer Fast Travel Games is a VR veteran and is hosting community challenges throughout the next few months, unlocking special loot when completed. The game has full crossplay support, so it should be very easy to find and invite friends, no matter which platform they prefer.

I played the standalone Quest 3 version, which looks quite nice visually and sports great performance. VR-specific mechanics are excellent, including on-body inventory, fun skill check-style minigames to keep equipment working, physical interaction with locks and other environmental details, and lots of shooting with physics-based gun mechanics. Hacking a computer while shooting in a completely different direction is something that only makes sense in VR, and I love leaning into those types of mechanics.

The biggest miss of Payday Aces High is not providing enough bespoke VR mechanics. You can’t grab characters, can’t break glass with a melee attack, and the stealth leaves a bit to be desired. I would love to be able to toss an object across the room to distract guards for a moment, for instance, as it would fit the medium very well.

But outside of a few immersion-breaking decisions like this, the action is incredibly fun, challenging, and, maybe most important of all, rewarding. My son and I have completed several missions many times over in hopes of perfecting the formula, and the increasing complexity and difficulty of each has proven to be an enjoyable challenge. Payday Aces High is my multiplayer VR game of the year, hands down, not just because I already loved the series, but because I find co-op multiplayer to be the most fun thing to do in a VR headset.

#10416. Three layers, one repo: a tour of google/adk-recipes

A developer building on Google Cloud today can scaffold a long-horizon memory agent from the terminal, install a retail vertical skill into a coding assistant with one line, and deploy a fully-wired production service to Cloud Run, Agent Runtime, Agent Garden, or Gemini Enterprise app — all from the same curated source tree. That tree is google/adk-recipes, the restructured successor to google/adk-samples that now serves Agent Platform from a single source of truth.

ADK recipes are agent-friendly: we've made it easy for your agents to 1) discover relevant recipes for various agent use cases, and 2) install and use them automatically from your favorite harness.

google/adk-recipes splits its content into three top-level folders: core/ for reference patterns curated by the Agents CLI and DevRel teams, plugins/ for installable vertical skills packaged as Plugin Recipes conforming to the Agent Plugins v1.0.0 spec, and contrib/ for end-to-end deployable services across five languages. Every piece has been designed with machine readability as the primary consideration.

What follows is a walkthrough of the repository's shape, three worked examples (one per layer), and a reflection on what the new structure enables.

Three top-level roots, each anchored on one reader action:

Every recipe ships a validated manifest.yaml, an AGENTS.md that tells the coding assistant opening it what the recipe is and how to use it, and (for contrib/ and plugins/) a Dockerfile plus a runnability test. CI enforces the standards on every PR, and a suite of coding-assistant skills in the repo (prepare-python-recipe, generate-manifest, github-pr-review, and others) does most of the preparation, so your agent can scaffold, validate, and submit a recipe end-to-end.

Every AGENTS.md, every manifest.yaml, every SKILL.md is written primarily for the AI coding assistant that will open the repo on your behalf; README.md files are what humans still read. The handbook glossary captures the dual framing: "Recipe — a runnable agent example (or importable agent module) under core/, contrib/, or plugins/, consumed by ADK developers and coding agents alike."

adk-recipes serves as the single source of truth for ADK content across the three Agent Platform surfaces that consume it. Each reads the repository through its own protocol.

Agents CLI reads core/. When a developer runs uvx --from google-agents-cli agents-cli create my-agent, Agents CLI can scaffold on demand a core/ recipe. The CLI, launched earlier this year at Google Cloud Next, exposes the full scaffold → deploy → evaluate → publish lifecycle; the base content it serves comes from our recipes. Horizon, the reference harness we examine below, is an example pattern Agents CLI treats as a production template.

Agent Garden features a curated selection. The developer-facing path into the repository is a pull request to contrib/: a recipe lands in GitHub, becomes part of the public ADK ecosystem, and from that moment is discoverable by other developers and by coding assistants reading the repository. Appearing in the Agent Garden gallery is a separate editorial decision made by the Agent Garden team, which curates a subset of recipes and promotes them to the public catalog on their own cadence. Once an agent is in the gallery, any developer in a Google Cloud project gets a one-click install.

Beyond these primary surfaces, Google Cloud's own infrastructure components appear as building blocks inside the recipes themselves. Horizon uses Agent Platform Sandboxes for per-user sandboxing, Memory Bank for durable memory, and Agent Platform Sessions for session state. The retail product-search Plugin Recipe uses Vector Search 2.0 on Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform paired with BigQuery. The retail virtual-tryon Plugin Recipe uses Gemini image models for still composites and Veo 3.1 Reference-to-Video for animated outputs. The recipes do not just live alongside Agent Platform; they document it in use.

For developers working with coding assistants outside Google Cloud's tooling (Claude Code, Antigravity, Cursor, Windsurf, Zed, and others), Plugin Recipes additionally install through the open npx skills CLI maintained by vercel-labs.

Building an agent that can handle a single conversation is a well-understood problem. Building one that holds its own across days of interaction, remembering what the user did yesterday and planning tasks for tomorrow, is a materially different undertaking. The hard parts of such a long-horizon system are the same regardless of what the agent is being built to do: cross-session memory, per-user sandboxing of tool execution, secrets management, guardrails against runaway behaviour, and the orchestration primitives that make multi-step tasks resumable. Horizon, which lives at core/python/long-horizon-harness, is the reference harness we built to collect these primitives in one place.

The shortest path from a fresh clone to a running Horizon instance:

make deploy rolls the backend to Cloud Run through Terraform; make destroy tears it back down. Horizon's evaluation suite lives in tests/eval/evalsets/ as eighteen named behavioural rubrics covering memory recall, injection-based exfiltration attempts, guardrail halting under adversarial input, tool-selection discipline, and a journey-level plot-artifact evaluation. The rubrics are executed with uv run pytest tests/eval.

A first chat turn against the running backend confirms the memory layer is wired correctly:

Horizon is the harness we give coding assistants to study before building something harder. When a developer asks their assistant to build an agent that must run across days of interaction, the right next step is uvx google-agents-cli create with Horizon as the scaffold, and the rest of the harness falls into place.

Horizon encodes a reference pattern. It does not encode a vertical. For that, the second layer of the repository holds Plugin Recipes, each addressing a specific industry domain. The first vertical fully covered in this launch is retail, available under plugins/retail/.

The Plugin Recipe format sits on top of the Agent Plugins v1.0.0 specification published at agent-plugins.org, an open standard for packaging installable AI skills across coding assistants. The specification defines a plugin as a bundle of one or more skills with a top-level plugin.json manifest declaring name, version, license, and ownership. Each skill is a self-contained folder with a SKILL.md conversational installer, a README.md, an EVAL.yaml of quality gates, and the scripts and assets the skill needs to do its work. The retail plugin's layout is:

Two skills ship in this plugin today. The first, retail-product-search, builds an ADK agent with a vector-search retrieval tool backed by Vector Search 2.0 on the Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform and a BigQuery catalog. The skill handles the entire pipeline: ingesting a CSV or JSON catalog into BigQuery, generating multimodal and text embeddings with Vertex AI Embeddings, building the Vector Search collection, scaffolding the ADK agent with a retrieve_docs tool, and deploying to Cloud Run. The agent is deliberately single-agent: one root_agent with one retrieval tool and no coordinator layer. The semantic complexity sits in the retrieval pipeline and the evaluator rubric, not in agent-to-agent communication; adding orchestration layers would obscure the pattern rather than clarify it.

The second skill, retail-virtual-tryon, generates a photorealistic composite of a model wearing or holding a product, with an optional eight-second studio catwalk video on top. It uses gemini-2.5-flash-image by default, falling back to gemini-2.5-pro-image when editorial quality matters more than latency. For video it uses veo-3.1-generate-001 in Reference-to-Video mode, conditioning the generated motion on the still produced by the image pipeline. The skill ships a pre-flight product-cutout classifier and configurable safety levels, operational details that become obvious after the third real production deployment.

Both skills install into any coding assistant that reads skills with a single command:

The --full-depth flag instructs the CLI to walk past its default top-level lookup and discover the nested plugins/retail/skills/ structure. The install lands in ~/.agents/skills// and is symlinked into every detected coding assistant on the local machine:

The skill ecosystem is designed to compose. The retail-product-search SKILL.md instructs the host coding assistant to pull two companion skills from google/skills if they are not already installed, keeping cross-cutting concerns like BigQuery basics and Gemini API usage in one place and reusable by multiple vertical plugins.

Once installed, the coding assistant reads SKILL.md and initiates a Q-MODE interview. For retail-product-search, Quick Start is two questions (GCP project ID and the path to product data, with sensible defaults for both); Full Setup adds two more covering product-fields level and GCP region. By the end of the interview the skill has written a ./design-spec.md reflecting the project's actual shape and executed scripts/setup.py to land the catalog in BigQuery, generate the embeddings, build the Vector Search collection, and run EVAL.yaml against the result. If the rubric and assertion checks fall below the 80 percent passing target, the skill halts and surfaces the specific failures.

An end-to-end setup run on the bundled five-product demo catalog produces:

A live retrieval against the collection the skill has just provisioned returns:

The virtual-tryon skill is faster to demonstrate interactively. A direct call to generate_tryon_image with a user portrait and a product image produces the photorealistic composite shown at the top of this section in roughly ten seconds. The same inputs piped through generate_tryon_video and Veo Reference-to-Video turn that still into an eight-second studio catwalk animation of the composite in motion.

The pipeline is category-agnostic. Swapping the product image for a pair of sunglasses, with product_category='eyewear', invokes the same generate_tryon_image call and produces a composite in the correct category without any further configuration:

Retail is the first vertical fully covered in this format. Additional verticals will land as their own folders under plugins/, following the same anatomy and the same Agent Plugins specification, so developers can expect a consistent install experience across industries.

The contrib/ layer is where ADK recipes become end-to-end deployable services. Twelve Python agents live there today, spanning the kinds of applications ADK is typically used to build in practice: finance (financial advisor, small-business loan processor), evaluation (LLM auditor), document and knowledge pipelines (high-volume document analyzer, multiformat hybrid RAG), content and brand (brand-aligned presentations, brand-search optimization, on-brand genmedia), retail (product search, virtual try-on), and framework examples (agent-skills tutorial, staged UI widgets). Java, Go, TypeScript, and Kotlin each carry smaller sets. Every entry in contrib/ is a worked example of how a core/ pattern or a plugins/ skill becomes a running service.

To make the relationship between a plugin and its deployable counterpart concrete, take contrib/python/retail-product-search as the example. The retail plugin from the previous section walked a developer through a conversational install that provisioned the Vector Search collection, the BigQuery catalog, and the embeddings. The contrib agent is the deployable counterpart: a long-running service that reads from that collection and answers natural-language shopping queries over it. The plugin owns the one-shot setup; the contrib agent owns the service shape. Both are maintained by the same team to stay synchronised, and the agent's README directs new users to run the plugin's setup first:

Once the collection (retail-skill-products-collection) is populated, the agent is cloned out of contrib/python/retail-product-search, pointed at a GCP project, and surfaced on one of five deployment personas controlled by boolean flags in .env.

Once the deployment is live, the agent exposes a hosted shopping-assistant endpoint. The same retrieve_docs tool we called directly from Python now sits behind a chat interface, reachable through the deployed Reasoning Engine or, during development, through the ADK Web UI. The exact same agent code answering the exact same shopping query, run locally for inspection:

The agent graph on the left of the UI confirms the single-agent architecture (product_search_agent → retrieve_docs). The right panel traces each tool invocation. The response grounds in a real catalog row from Vector Search: "Sony Wireless Headphones · Price: $199.99 · Brand: Sony · Rating: 4.5/5 (50 in stock) · Premium noise-cancelling over-ear headphones featuring an impressive 30-hour battery life." No products are hallucinated; the Vector Search collection on top of BigQuery is the source of truth.

That is what the retail plugin gives a team. The plugin encodes the data-pipeline setup once, through a conversational install any coding assistant can execute. The contrib agent is the running service, deployable in five shapes, that any organisation can take as a starting point and swap in its own catalog. The virtual-tryon counterpart in contrib/python/retail-virtual-tryon follows the same pattern: a smaller root agent that orchestrates try_on_product_image and try_on_product_video tools, deployable in the same five shapes, built on the virtual-tryon plugin's one-shot setup.

A reference harness without a vertical is a toy; a vertical skill without a deployable artifact is a slide; a deployable agent without a reference pattern is a prototype no one can repeat. Shipping all three in one repository, under a shared manifest schema and governance pipeline, lets a developer or a coding assistant walk the stack in either direction, with every Agent Platform surface reading from the same source of truth.

The practical result is a lower barrier from "I need an agent that does X" to a running production service: a coding assistant can scaffold a Horizon-shaped agent, install a vertical Plugin Recipe, and reach the deployment transcripts in contrib/ without translating between repositories or reconciling manifest formats. For teams building vertical solutions, Plugin Recipes package domain expertise once and distribute it to every coding assistant on the market with the same install flow and quality gates.

Not an officially supported Google product. Recipes are for demonstration and as starting points, not production use.

#10415. LG WOLED TVs suffer from 'edge rot' pixel death

User reports of the problem keep mounting, as well, including from RTings staff and our own.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

When shopping for an OLED TV, most prospective owners are concerned about burn-in, a problem that's been mostly mitigated, save perhaps for scenarios that involve leaving a TV on 24/7. This has been demonstrated by RTing's famous long-running test, but the site has found a new problem that seemingly pertains to pre-2023 LG WOLED panels: edge rot, where pixels progressively go dark around the edges, similar to a mold pattern.

RTings says that 14 out of the 16 LG televisions in its burn-in test have developed this problem in varying degrees, so 87.5% of a small test sample are affected. Those sets were all made between 2020 and 2022. The site says that several staff members saw the issue pop up on their personal televisions. Some searches indicate similar reports from users everywhere, and coincidentally, I learned about this just last week as a close friend sent photos of his 2019 LG TV.

The site says that the television sets bearing Samsung QDOLED panels in its burn-in test do not exhibit these symptoms, though it notes that it only has three such units in its test run. Additionally, since the test ran between 2022 and 2025, RTings says it can't assess whether LG's newer-generation WOLED panels have this issue, and notes that the manufacturing process for said panels reportedly improved in 2024.

Needless to say, most of the television sets in the study are going to be out of warranty. LG does have five-year panel warranty coverage, but only since 2021, and only covering higher-end units like the G, Z, and M series. It's still worth looking into this, in case you own a set from those lines and within the covered timeframe.

RTings asked for statements on the matter from both LG and Samsung, and interestingly, both firms discussed moisture directly or indirectly. LG stated that the issue "may occur in a very small number of OLED products under prolonged exposure to high-temperature, high-humidity, or other harsh environmental conditions."

It's not likely that RTing's test environment is especially humid, though, and the flurry of user reports with TVs sitting in their own living rooms doesn't lend much credence to LG's argument.

Meanwhile, Samsung is more specific, saying that it's implemented its proprietary Thin Film Encapsulation (TFE), a method for blocking ingress at the panel's edge. The firm says TFE "[eliminates] the root cause of border degradation and [ensures] long-term edge-to-edge uniformity where traditional structures falter."

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

RTings says that according to conversations with industry experts, "contamination during manufacturing, defects in the encapsulation of some OLED displays, or degradation of that encapsulation over time" may be at the source of the problem. The site's investigation also notes that some of its test units are affected by edge delamination.

The publication also believes the pixel-death problem may be related to power-on hours, while edge delamination could be dependent on the unit's age. It's unlikely that all user reports come in from people leaving their TVs on 24/7 or close to it, though. A sample size of one means little, but the TV is used sparingly at my friend's home, only around 30 hours per week — over nearly seven years, he's only seen the pixel refresh function come up twice.

For whatever it's worth, he notes that his set's dark spots mostly reside on the side closest to a portion of the wall with very minor humidity ingress from outside the building. The set is resting on furniture, not wall-mounted, though, so the relation between humidity and the edge rot is inconclusive at best.

#10405. How to Use CSS Attribute Selectors to Build More Flexible User Interfaces

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When developing a website, developers often rely on CSS classes to style buttons, forms, navigation elements, and other interface components. This approach works well, but some situations require styles to depend on an element's HTML attributes rather than additional class names.

CSS attribute selectors provide a practical solution. They allow developers to target elements based on whether an attribute exists, what value it contains, or whether its value matches a particular pattern. Used carefully, they can simplify styling and make reusable components easier to maintain.

An attribute selector targets an HTML element using square brackets. For example, a developer can select every input field that has a required attribute:

This rule applies to inputs with the required attribute, regardless of their input type.

Developers can also target a specific attribute value:

This is useful when different input types need different visual treatments without introducing additional classes into the HTML.

Forms are a practical place to apply attribute selectors because HTML already provides attributes that describe how individual fields behave.

Consider a registration form containing email, password, and telephone fields. Each input can be styled according to its purpose:

The CSS can target these fields independently:

This approach keeps the markup descriptive while allowing the stylesheet to respond to the attributes already present in the document.

One important distinction is that required indicates a validation requirement, while :invalid represents an input's current validity state. These should not be treated as interchangeable.

CSS provides several matching operators that help developers target more specific attribute values.

These operators can be useful when working with links, downloadable files, language attributes, and reusable components.

For example, a website might want to distinguish PDF downloads from ordinary navigation links:

This adds a small visual indicator to links whose URLs end in .pdf.

Attribute selectors can also help identify links with different destinations or behaviors.

For example, external links that use HTTPS can receive a subtle visual indicator:

However, matching an HTTPS URL does not necessarily prove that a link points to an external website. Internal links can also use HTTPS, so developers should consider the actual navigation requirements before applying special styling.

For more examples of selector combinations and matching rules, SitePoint's guide to CSS Attribute Selectors provides a useful reference.

Although attribute selectors are flexible, they should not replace every class-based styling decision.

A selector such as [class*="button"] can match unintended elements if their class names happen to contain the same text. Similarly, broad attribute rules may affect components that were not originally intended to receive those styles.

For predictable results, use exact attribute matching whenever possible and prefer descriptive classes for major component styling.

Developers should also remember that CSS selectors only control presentation. They do not replace JavaScript validation, server-side security checks, or proper HTML semantics.

The MDN documentation on attribute selectors provides further details about selector syntax, matching behavior, and supported operators.

Before adding attribute selectors to a large stylesheet, inspect the affected elements using browser developer tools.

Check whether the selector matches the intended elements.

Test fields with different attribute values.

Verify disabled, required, and invalid form states.

Check navigation links and downloadable files.

Test the layout on mobile and desktop screens.

Review whether existing component styles are being unintentionally overridden.

This process helps identify unexpected matches before they affect the production interface.

CSS attribute selectors give developers another way to create flexible and maintainable interfaces without adding unnecessary classes to every HTML element.

They are particularly useful for form styling, link identification, and components that already contain meaningful HTML attributes. By choosing precise selectors, understanding matching operators, and testing changes before deployment, developers can use this feature to write cleaner CSS while keeping their markup descriptive and organized.

Radeon drivers may be quietly racking up thousands of gigabytes in SSD writes

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

An SSD can spend most of its day doing very little, particularly when a PC is sitting idle. That may not be the case for some AMD Radeon systems, however, with reports suggesting that AMD’s External Events Utility (EEU), which is installed alongside Radeon drivers, may be writing data to the system driver continuously, even when the computer is barely being used. The issue drew attention again on November 8, when Reddit user ‘darklinkpower’ highlighted reports of the unusual drive activity.

One Radeon owner claimed the problem had been quietly eating into their drive's reported health for more than a year, writing: "I just found out that the driver eats up 1% of my SSD health every month for 16 months." Another user reported sustained writes of approximately 5MB/s while their PC was idle. Another one claimed their SSD health had dropped by about 1% every month for 16 months. AMD has acknowledged the problem. In an October 21 response on its community forum, a company representative confirmed that they were investigating. “Yes. We're looking into it with the aim to resolve it in a future driver release." He said. Another representative, AMAD Vik, subsequently offered a more encouraging update on Reddit, apologizing and saying, “This is hopefully fixed in the next one."

Notably, the EEU is not an entirely unfamiliar culprit. Last November, a similar issue involving AMD chipset drivers arose, with a Redditor claiming he had discovered that the software repeatedly updated a log file whenever on-screen elements were moved or resized. That incident also prompted questions about whether stopping the service would affect FreeSync. The latest reports are a different proposition, though, with some users now seeing writes that barely let up, even when their PCs are doing nothing.

The numbers are worth looking at. At 5 MB/s, an SSD would receive about 18GB of host writes per hour. Over a full day, that's 432GB, and a month of uninterrupted activity could push the figures close to 13TB. That, however, does not necessarily mean being written to the NAND flash itself. SSD controllers use caching and wear-leveling, and other techniques, to manage incoming data. How much of that reported activity thus translates into actual flash wear is still unknown. The user's claim of losing 1% of drive health each month also hasn't been independently confirmed.

Moreover, there doesn't appear to be clear patterns across Radeon driver versions. Reports include systems running Adrenalin 26.19.2, as well as older releases, suggesting the problem may not be limited to the latest update. AMD's October 30 release notes for Adrenalin 26.20.2 don't mention the issue either. For now, it's difficult to say which driver versions, hardware combinations, or system configurations are responsible.

That said, there are two reported workarounds. The first involves opening the Windows Services application by pressing Win + R, entering services.msc, and locating AMD EEU. Users can then stop the service or change its startup type to Disabled. That appears to stop excessive writeups, but isn’t necessarily a clean solution. The second workaround involves disabling Resizable Bar (ReBAR), which another user reported had stopped the disk activity without disabling the EEU. This, however, isn’t ideal either, as ReBAR can improve gaming performance by allowing the PC to access the GPU’S memory more efficiently.

For now, checking whether the problem actually exists on your PC is probably the most sensible first step. Open Windows Resource Monitor by pressing Win + R, typing resmon, and selecting the Disk tab. Sort by write activity and look for sustained writes from atieclxx.exe or atiesrxx.exe. AMD has indicated that a fix is in the works, but hasn’t published a confirmed release date. Until then, Radeon owners may want to monitor their storage activity rather than immediately disabling driver services or changing BIOS settings.

#10399. Native Model Authoring for Edge with LiteRT Tensor API

Shipping on-device AI means meeting tight latency and memory budgets on every device you target. The most common path for deploying models in production is converting a trained model, for example converting a PyTorch model with LiteRT Torch, and running it with the LiteRT runtime. This offers the quickest and most reliable route to production, and it's the path we recommend for most projects. Sometimes, though, developers want full control over how a network is represented and optimized to support new architectures or to try a new lowering logic without waiting for upstream framework updates.

For ultimate control, developers write computations manually in native C++ or shader languages. Apple's MLX made this code-first approach popular on Apple silicon, and llama.cpp showed how far tailored C++ implementations can take LLM inference. However, the level of control comes at a significant cost: implementations are tightly coupled to specific backends (such as Metal, OpenCL, or Vulkan) and must be rewritten for each new hardware target. Furthermore, managing zero-copy buffer interoperability and synchronization across CPU, GPU, and NPU, while gluing together pre-processing, inference, and post-processing, demands substantial manual engineering effort.

To solve the above challenges, we are excited to announce the release of the novel LiteRT Tensor API, a lightweight authoring layer in the LiteRT stack, designed to improve handcrafting models. It combines the flexibility of native authoring with cross-platform portability. You build the computation graph op by op in C++, choosing exactly which ops run and how they connect. The same graph can run through LiteRT on CPU, GPU (powered by open-source ML Drift) and NPU, or without model file or runtime directly on CPU through XNNPACK, so you don't rewrite it for each new hardware target. Model inference and pre/post-processing can be assembled with the same API and chained through shared accelerator buffers, so that data never round-trips through the CPU. Start with the conversion path, which remains the best default for most models. Reach for the Tensor API when you need low-level control over the graph or want the whole pipeline to run end to end on the accelerator.

The LiteRT Tensor API also serves as an ideal programmatic foundation for AI coding agents, providing structured primitives to generate, rewire, and tune model graphs automatically. In the rest of this post, we’ll dive into the design and walk through practical code examples showing how to author models from scratch.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

To show the Tensor API in practice, we built an interactive Segment Anything App, running entirely on-device in the browser on WebGPU. The app enables real-time multi-object segmentation and tracking via Meta's Segment Anything Model 2 (SAM 2), triggered either by simple clicks or natural language prompts powered by Gemma 4 E2B.

The SAM 2 model is fully authored in C++ via the LiteRT Tensor API. A coding agent wrote and evolved it from under 10 to over 30 frames per second at 512x512 on WebGPU. See that the complete implementation and the web app are open source in the LiteRT Samples GitHub repo.

The LiteRT Tensor API provides four key features for authoring and optimizing on-device models:

We ran the same C++ code for the SAM 2 model on the GPUs of three devices: a macOS laptop (Metal), an iOS phone (Metal), and an Android phone (OpenCL). In our tests on the MacBook Pro, it ran about 1.5x faster than Apple MLX (fp16, image encoder plus mask decoder). The SAM 2.1 sample includes the benchmark commands so you can reproduce the results.

With the Tensor API, each layer is a C++ function over Tensor values, built from high-level operators and weights loaded directly from the checkpoint. For example, here is a multi-head self-attention layer:

In the SAM 2 sample, BuildEncoder and BuildDecoder compose layers like this into the encoder and decoder graphs, and ModelFactory exports both as two signatures of one sam2_image.tflite file.

A .tflite file is one of two ways to run your graph. The backend is a template argument on Tensor: TfLiteMixinTag exports a .tflite that LiteRT runs on CPU, ML Drift (GPU), or NPU, and XnnpackMixinTag runs the same code directly on CPU through XNNPACK, with no model file. Tag tensors with both to keep both options:

SAM 2 takes the .tflite path to reach WebGPU in the browser, and the rest of this post follows it. The Gemma 4 example takes the direct path for prefill and decode on CPU, the lightest way to run Tensor API code. The Tensor API README walks through both.

A natural workflow uses both: iterate on the direct XNNPACK path, loading weights straight from the .safetensors checkpoint with no model file to rebuild, then export a .tflite when you're ready to ship with LiteRT on mobile or on the web through LiteRT.js.

A real app needs more than the neural network itself. In the demo, turning a camera frame and a click into a mask takes four steps: pre-processing converts the frame into the 512x512 image the model expects; the image encoder turns that image into features; the mask decoder combines those features with the click to predict a mask; and post-processing scales the mask back to the frame's size. Running this whole pipeline fast takes two things: keeping data on the accelerator (such as the GPU) between steps, and controlling how each step runs on each accelerator.

In a typical app, only the model runs on the GPU; the CPU resizes each camera frame, passes the encoder's output to the decoder, and post-processes the mask. Each trip copies data and stalls the GPU.

In litert-samples/sam2, all four steps are Tensor API graphs on the GPU. Pre-processing, for example, is a small graph built with CreateLambdaRunner. The graphs are connected once at startup to share buffers, then run for each frame and click:

No data returns to the CPU between steps: all 522 encoder and 269 decoder operations run on the GPU, and pre- and post-processing add only about 0.34 ms per frame.

Because the graph is plain C++, you or your coding agent can rewrite it for each backend. Our agent, Antigravity, moved SAM 2's per-frame decisions, such as whether the object is still visible, from CPU code into the graph. Comparisons like score > 0 would still fall back to the CPU on WebGPU, because they output true/false values, so the agent rewrote them as arithmetic in StepPos:

Each object now takes a single GPU call per frame. The agent made a series of changes like this, such as folding the attention scale into the weights, and checked each against Hugging Face's SAM 2 before keeping it. That's how we more than tripled the model's frame rate.

We're working on direct ML Drift (GPU) backends, NPU-friendly operations, and automatic full-graph quantization. We're also adding Python authoring with .tflite export, plus wider dynamic-shape support for varying image sizes, batch sizes, and sequence lengths.

Explore our resources and start your journey with LiteRT:

The LiteRT Tensor API and its reference implementations are the result of a collaborative effort across the Google AI Edge and LiteRT teams. Special thanks to Byungchul Kim, Changming Sun, Chenchen Tang, Dillon Sharlet, Jingjiang Li, Lu Wang, Lutz Justen, Matthias Grundmann, Misha Gutman, Ronald Wotzlaw, and Sachin Kotwani.

What is Pathways? Single-controller training for large models

#10398. I bought the knock-off Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air bands, and it’s crazy how good they are [Gallery]

I love swapping out the bands on my Pixel Watch 5 and Fitbit Air, but Google’s official options are not cheap. It doesn’t take long to find a plethora of knock-off Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air bands around the web and, as it turns out, they’re actually really good.

Over the past few generations, I’ve collected a ton of Pixel Watch bands and, at first, that was almost entirely first-party bands. For the first couple of years of Pixel Watch, third-party bands were either non-existent or of terrible quality, but they’ve gotten a lot better.

Last year, I showcased some knock-off Active Sport bands that, for $8, very effectively filled the gap for me while Google’s lineup was lacking some colors I wanted.

Since then, I’ve tried others. The most recent batch is a 6-pack of Pixel Watch bands that are copies of Google’s standard “Active” band. They’re made from silicone, not the fluorelastomer from past generations, nor the “Hydrogenated Nitrile Butadiene Rubber (HNBR)” used on Pixel Watch 5. But, in action, it gets the job done.

The bands sit somewhere between Google’s small and large sizes and slot into the Pixel Watch 5’s mechanism perfectly. If you handed this to me and said it was an official band from Google, I’d probably believe you.

To get this level of quality at such a low price is wild, all while getting colors that, again, Google doesn’t make. I found this while just wanting a purple band, but the price tag pushed me to just buy a whole pack, and the striking deep red included sneakily became one of my favorites.

Pixel Watch isn’t alone here – third-party knock-off bands for Fitbit Air are very good, as my colleage Damien Wilde has pointed out repeatedly.

I originally bought the Stephen Curry edition Fitbit Air and, long story short, I don’t have that official band anymore. But the $9 (currently even cheaper!) knock-off I bought on Amazon is quite literally identical in quality, feel, and even the details. The only thing it’s missing is the Stephen Curry badge (which, frankly, I don’t miss anyway), but it still has that little piece of added material where the wordmark would have been.

The same goes for the matching band I picked up for my Pixel Watch 5.

Google doesn’t sell this band on its own, which is annoying, but the knock-off I bought is basically identical to what I felt during our hands-on with the official one back in August, though this one doesn’t have the space for the Stephen Curry badge the real one does. The lugs connecting to the watch are also different and not nearly as stylish, but I’m absolutely not complaining here.

Is it possible that Google’s supply chain is producing these near-identical versions? Given the quality at play, I really wouldn’t be surprised! That said, a lot of these are simple copies. It’s not all that hard to spin up generic versions of products like these.

Most third party cheap Fitbit Air bands I've tried range from "decent" to "very good".

Most third party cheap and expensive Pixel Watch bands I've tried range from "very, very bad" to "almost decent".

The major issue with PW 3rd party bands isn't the band itself. It's the connecting lugs.

Not how they look, but how they're manufactured. High tolerances, rattling, too lose or too tight connections.

And that applies also to certified "made for Google" bands (i.e. the Spigen ones).

I've given up. I'm going to buy the official Pixel Watch leather band and take the official lugs out of it, so I can use with any 22mm strap.

I’ll still happily pick up some of Google’s official bands because it’s hard to beat some of those color options but, for the most part, it’s getting hard to go wrong with the knock-off Pixel Watch bands, and even more so with the Fitbit Air. The simpler tracker gets even better third-party options seeing as Google made the mounting mechanism free for everyone.

Whatever the case, third-party / knock-off Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air bands are in a really good place right now, and that’s refreshing after a few years of using this lineup every day.

"We agree users should be explicitly informed"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Interaxon, the Canadian firm behind the Muse sleep and meditation headband, is one of them. Its devices monitor electrical activity in the brain, alongside other physiological measurements, while users sleep. The recordings are processed into reports that help customers track their rest and identify changes in their sleeping patterns. But there is another use for that information. Interaxon has already trained an AI model using about 50,000 hours of EEG recording and says it removes identifying information, including names, email, and addresses, before using the data. “We aim to use as much of that eligible corpus as possible,” Nadia Kumentas, its Chief Marketing Officer, said.

Notably, the idea behind these systems is particularly different from training a large language model. Instead of feeding AI an enormous amount of text, developers supply it with recordings of electrical activity in the brain. The model then learns recurring patterns that can be adapted to recognize different brain states or assist with specific research tasks. And there are already some interesting results. AI startup Piramidal has trained its model on more than 4 million hours of EEG data in partnership with Cleveland Clinic. The company developed a seizure-detection system that correctly identified nine out of 10 seizures in a pilot study after being fine-tuned on the clinic's recordings. That, however, doesn’t mean consumer headbands can suddenly read people's thoughts. Most wearable EEG devices use just one to six electrodes, considerably fewer than 19 to 25 typically found in clinical EEG systems. Also, their recordings are generally less comprehensive.

The industry has also been gathering neural recordings on a considerable scale. Last December, we covered Coundit’s effort to collect 10,000 hours of neural data from thousands of patients. The San Francisco startup built its own headset system to capture brain activity alongside speech and text, with the aim of developing AI capable of translating signals into language. Meta has also been working on BrainQwerty, a non-invasive brain-computer interface that translates brain activity into keyboard inputs. Its second-generation system improved average word accuracy to 61% from 40% in the earlier version, although the technology remains a far cry from practical everyday use.

There aren't many options for gaming that beat the AMD Ryzen 7 9800X3D. It's a powerhouse that was, until recently, the best CPU for gaming in the world, beating every other chip due to its boosted L3 cache capacity. You're getting an eight-core, 16-thread processor, made using AMD's current-gen Zen 5 architecture, with a boost clock speed of 5.2 GHz, and a 120W TDP.

The 9800X3D is fully unlocked, so it's possible to overclock it, which was a limitation in older 3D V-cache chips from AMD. It's that extra L3 cache that makes this such a special chip, too, stacked beneath the CPU cores closer to the heat spreader on a reconfigured die.

This 8-core X3D beast of a processor is on sale at its lowest ever price. While the Ryzen 7 9850X3D is faster by around 3%, the benchmarks prove how close of a run it is, with this CPU requiring 30% more power for almost the same performance.

Faster chips do technically exist, like the AMD Ryzen 7 9850X3D, which our CPU benchmark data shows is 3% faster at 1080p than the 9800X3D, although with much higher power demands. The 9800X3D offers the best performance per watt of any other gaming CPU, so you can really maximise performance without a huge increase in electrical power. You can see, too, from the Bench charts below that the 9800X3D sits very close to the top as far as CPU performance goes.

Put simply, if you pair this processor with a recent GPU, you won't see many bottlenecks. Pair it with a top-class option like the RTX 5080 or RTX 5090, and you'll be hitting high frame rates in any game you can throw at it. CPU-intensive games like Cyberpunk 2077 won't be let down by that 96MB of L3 cache included in this X3D chip.

Given how good a processor this is, and how little Intel has been able to rival it, we can't see it falling out of date for a long while yet. It's sitting at the top of the food chain and will continue to dominate games for years into the future. AMD's AM5 platform, meanwhile, remains in use, and AMD has promised to support it for at least one more generation.

#10391. How to Get a YouTube API Key and Use the Data API (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You need a YouTube API key the moment your app has to read anything from YouTube: a video's length, its view count, a channel's latest uploads. Getting one takes about five minutes. Using it well takes a little longer, because YouTube changed how its quota works in June 2026, and plenty of guides still describe the old rules.

In this guide we'll get a key, lock it down and test it. Then we'll use it from Node.js for three real jobs: adding up the length of a playlist, listing a channel's videos without spending a search, and estimating what a video earned. Along the way we'll cover what the YouTube API costs (nothing), how the 2026 quota works, and the errors you're most likely to hit.

A YouTube API key identifies your project to the YouTube Data API v3. With just a key, you can read public data:

A key isn't enough for anything that touches someone's account. Uploading videos, editing playlists or reading private data needs OAuth 2.0, where the user signs in and grants your app access.

You'll need a Google account. The YouTube Data API is free, so there's nothing to pay for.

The restrictions matter more than they look. A key is just a string in a URL, and anyone who copies an unrestricted one can spend your daily quota for you. With an API restriction, the key is useless for other Google APIs. With an application restriction, it only works from your server or your site. Changes to restrictions can take up to five minutes to apply, so don't panic if a request fails straight after you save.

Before writing any code, check the key with a single request. This one asks for the first video ever uploaded to YouTube, and uses the fields parameter to trim the response down to four values:

That request cost 1 unit of your daily quota, which brings us to the question everyone asks.

Yes. There's no charge per request and no paid plan. Instead you get a daily quota, and since June 1, 2026 it's split into separate buckets:

Google's quota calculator lists the cost of every method. Quotas reset at midnight Pacific Time. Every request costs at least 1 unit, including requests that fail, so a retry loop around a broken call can use up a day's quota on its own.

If you've read older guides, search is the big change. A search used to cost 100 units out of the same 10,000, so 100 searches emptied your whole day. Uploads were worse still, at about 1,600 units each until December 2025. Now both have their own buckets, and the 10,000 units are left for everything else.

To give you a feel for what 10,000 units buys, here's what each example in this guide costs:

You can't pay for more. To go above the defaults, fill in the YouTube API Services Audit and Quota Extension Form, which is linked from Google's page on quota and compliance audits. YouTube first audits your project to check it follows the YouTube API Services Terms of Service, then someone from the API team gets back to you. Google doesn't give a timeline, so apply well before you need the extra quota.

Most projects never need to. The rest of this guide is about getting a lot done with very few units.

The examples need Node.js 20.6 or later, for the built-in fetch and the --env-file flag. There are no packages to install. Make a folder with a .env file in it:

Every example imports one small helper. It builds the URL, adds your key and turns API errors into readable messages:

Run any example with node --env-file=.env followed by the file name.

YouTube doesn't tell you how long a playlist is. To work it out, you need two methods:

Durations come back in ISO 8601 format. PT15M33S is 15 minutes 33 seconds, PT1H2M3S adds an hour, and a stream longer than a day looks like P1DT2H3M4S. The toSeconds() function below handles all three.

Here it is on freeCodeCamp's Full Courses in One Video playlist:

That's 184 hours of courses for 4 units of quota. Private and deleted videos still show up in a playlist's items, but videos.list doesn't return them, which is why the script prints "52 of 52 videos available". On a playlist with removed videos, the two numbers will differ.

This is the core of the free YouTube playlist length calculator I built on createthat.ai. The live version adds a target time box that works out the speed you need to finish by a deadline, and it accepts channel, video and Shorts links as well as playlists.

The obvious way to find a channel's videos is to search for them. Don't: search is now capped at 100 calls a day, and it isn't built to list everything a channel has posted. Every channel has an uploads playlist instead, and you can reach it in two cheap calls:

Two units, and none of your 100 daily searches. To get every upload rather than the newest five, page through the playlist with the same pageToken loop as Example 1.

videos.list with part=statistics returns a video's view, like and comment counts. Dislike counts have been private since December 2021 and favoriteCount is always 0, so don't build anything on those. The script also copes with a missing like or comment count, which you'll see on some videos.

This time the fields parameter trims the response to the values we use. That makes the response smaller, but it doesn't change the cost: the call is 1 unit either way.

Here it is on freeCodeCamp's Learn Python full course, one of the most-watched videos in that playlist:

Treat the last line as a rough range, not an answer. The Data API doesn't share anyone's revenue, so any estimate is views multiplied by an RPM: what a creator keeps per 1,000 views. RPM moves a lot with the topic and where the viewers live, and the estimate assumes the video shows ads, which not every channel does.

And sometimes you don't need an API at all. When I built a font tester, I needed a list of Google Fonts. The Google Fonts Developer API would have meant a key and a call on every visit, for a list that barely changes. Instead I generated a file of the 500 most popular fonts once, from Google's public font metadata, and the page loads it like any other file. No key, no quota and nothing to fail.

When a call fails, the response's error.message and error.details[0].reason tell you why. These are the ones you'll see most:

For the invalid key error, the full response looks like this:

Yes. Creating a key and calling the YouTube Data API costs nothing. You're limited by the daily quota instead, and there's no paid tier: more quota comes through an audit.

In the Google Cloud console, select your project and go to APIs & Services > Credentials. Your keys are listed under API keys, with a Show key option next to each one.

It works, but anyone can read the key from your page. If you do it, restrict the key to your website and to the YouTube Data API v3. A small server route that calls the API for you is safer, and it gives you somewhere to cache results.

By default, 100 searches, 100 uploads and 10,000 units for everything else. Most reads cost 1 unit and videos.list takes 50 IDs per call, so that's enough for the details of up to 500,000 videos a day.

Getting a YouTube API key is the easy part: one project, one API to enable, one key and two restrictions. The 2026 quota is generous if you work with it:

All three examples together cost 7 units, out of 10,000. From here, try commentThreads.list to pull a video's comments 100 at a time, or extend Example 2 to page through a channel's entire upload history and add it all up with the code from Example 1.

Goodbye, Wintel? Software for Windows too?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Huawei has introduced its first desktop PCs powered by its own 10-core processor as well as proprietary HarmonyOS operating system, reports the Southern China Morning Post. The company claims that the CPU and the OS are built entirely of using 'components manufactured' in China, which matters when it comes national security and domestic supply chains inside the sanctioned country. But whether these machines are truly 'entirely domestic' is an open question.

The Huawei Qinyun HM650 and HM650g desktop PCs come with 16 GB of LPDDR5 memory (probably made by China's CXMT) and either a 256GB or 512GB SSD (probably made by Chinese flash maker YMTC) are powered by the company's Kirin X90 system-on-chip, which originally debuted in Huawei's consumer systems in 2025. The key ingedient of the system it its processor, which features 10 Huawei-designed Armv8-based Taishan CPU cores in a 4+4+2 configuration, with all cores supporting simultaneous multithreading (SMT) to execute up to 20 threads simultameusly. The chip also integrates Huawei's Maleoon graphics processing unit (Arm Mali-based) and a dedicated neural processing unit for AI workloads.

Although Huawei doesn't officially disclose the Kirin X90's manufacturing process, a teardown analysis by TechInsights confirmed that the CPU is manufactured by SMIC using its 7nm-class N+2 process technology. This makes the X90 an example of Huawei's ability to develop PC-class processors using domestically available semiconductor manufacturing capabilities, albeit on a process technology that trails leading-edge nodes from Intel, TSMC, and Samsung.

The most interesting aspect of the Qinyun HM650 series is certainly Huawei's claim that all of its new system's components, including the processor, memory, storage, power supply, and wireless connectivity are manufactured domestically. Such certification itself does not necessarily mean that the entire semiconductor supply chain behind the machine is Chinese. While the CPU, memory, and storage chips are fabricated domestically, they are still made using foreign manufacturing equipment (from the Netherlands, U.S., and Japan), materials (from Japan), intellectual property (from the UK), and electronic design automation (EDA software from the U.S. and partly from China) tools.

Meanwhile, the claim about the operating system developed in China sounds ambitious. The machines run Huawei's HarmonyOS Enterprise Edition, which certainly reduces their dependence on foreign technology suppliers. Unlike conventional PCs that rely on Microsoft's Windows operating system, Huawei's desktops use a software platform developed and maintained by a Chinese company. Furthermore, unlike earlier HarmonyOS versions that incorporated Linux components, the HarmonyOS Enterprise Edition uses Huawei's own kernel and software architecture. This indeed gives Huawei greater control over operating system development, security updates, and integration with its hardware, although compatibility with existing Windows applications could remain a limitation for enterprise customers that rely on software by multinational corporations for Windows.

Keeping all these things in mind, can we say that Huawei's Qinyun HM650 series relies entirely on components developed and made in China? Probably yes. Yet, with major caveats, including a semiconductor supply chain that relies on tools and software designed and produced elsewhere. Furthermore, incompatibility of the OS with software designed for Windows could be a limitation for some customers.

#10384. Two more Garmin smartwatches are down to record-low prices

The Garmin Fenix E and Garmin Venu 3S are for two different types of people, but both are available at a surprisingly low price thanks to these limited-time Garmin deals.

Garmin watches are often expensive, which is why I always recommend them on sale. For example, the Garmin Fenix E, usually $799, is now down to $399. That’s the all-time low price for this model and probably the best Garmin deal I’ve seen so far this fall.

The Garmin Fenix E is essentially a Garmin Epix Gen 2 with a newer OS. It sports multi-GNSS navigation plus a 3-axis compass, gyroscope, and barometric altimeter that should cover basically all your tracking needs. It’s got pulse ox monitoring, respiration tracking, advanced sleep monitoring, real-time stamina tracking, and a training readiness score (so you know what days you should be taking it easy).

Despite all the features, this one will last you two weeks per charge and can utilize GPS for up to 42 hours on each charge. The stainless steel bezel looks great, and the 47mm fiber-reinforced polymer case is built to last. This is one of those watches that, quite frankly, makes no sense at full cost. At half the price, though? Yeah, that looks much better.

If you’re looking for something a little more elegant-looking, the Garmin Venu 3S is the right choice. At $299, this Garmin deal makes it $250 less than the Garmin Venu 4. You might think buying the newest one is automatically better, but that’s not necessarily the case between these two models.

For example, the Venu 4 is thicker and heavier because it uses an all-metal casing, has one less button on the side, lacks the permanent hour markers that are on the Venu 3S’s bezel, and has worse battery life by 1-2 days, depending on your usage. The Venu 4 has more features and a newer OS, but I personally like the OS design on the 3S better (and I’m apparently not alone on that front).

The Venu 4 also has new sleep guidance and coaching features, training load and several tools that used to be for Garmin Forerunner watches, plus lifestyle logging, but I’m not sure I’d want to pay $250 to get those. Either way, you’ve got a great option now that the Venu 3S is at such a fantastic price.

#10381. Discord is about to get way, way faster on your Android phone

A new Discord client built in Rust is looking pretty shiny and clean, even on low-end Android phones.

I love Discord, but if there’s one thing the app isn’t known for is it’s speed. That’s particularly true on phones that don’t have much RAM, but an upcoming new Discord client is changing the game for all users, regardless of the platform.

Discord CTO and co-founder Stanislav Vishnevskiy posted on X about several new Discord updates that are getting users excited. In the video, embedded below, you can see Discord running on two very old, lower-end phones (looks like a Samsung A-series to me). The current client, on the left, takes a full 3 seconds to launch, and then another 2 seconds to fully load. The new client, on the right, launches in under 1 second and is finished loading everything before the 2-second mark.

A release date has not been announced for the new client, although it is presumably launching soon.

We didn't forget about mobile, especially Android. Inspired by React Native, it drives the OS-native view layer through the same Rust codebase as the new desktop app. I think it might be the most responsive app on my phone now? https://t.co/uf5EwUtQLh pic.twitter.com/F8QCedZcJj

Free Bash script files your details, rotating through eight subject lines, 10 humorous message boilerplates, and six signoffs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A new GitHub repository dubbed ram-altman features a script to monitor your PC’s RAM usage and report back to the eponymous OpenAI boss. Sam Altman infamously described people having spare, underutilized DRAM in computers at home as “quite terrible” in a recent podcast. Thus, developer Iain Kay was inspired to write a bash script that emails the OpenAI boss once a day to report on any RAM that is underutilized. Clearly this is not a serious tool, or rather “The memory accounting is. The correspondence isn't,” says Kay.

ram-altman leans on the MemAvailable query, which returns the kernel's own estimate of how much memory programs can claim without swapping, explains the developer of this script. When installed, it checks every five minutes and emails Altman once a day if you are found to be wasting this precious resource.

The emails take the .CSV file logs of your RAM use throughout the day as the data source to compose a summary email. These script-generated messages rotate through eight subject lines, 10 humorous message boilerplates, and six signoffs. Sample emails can be seen on the ram-altman.lol website. Altman’s email address isn’t supplied as part of the script.

If your machine is using 90% or more of its RAM, then you won’t be reported to the OpenAI boss for wasting memory. Also, if you don’t want to disturb Altman, you can run the tests without emailing anyone with the terminal command: bash ram-altman.sh --dry-run

The developer isn’t too scathing of the OpenAI boss. Kay quotes Altman’s explanations that his remarks were taken out of context. Moreover, the dev doffs his cap to the artificial intelligence leader’s humorous display name change to ‘Ram Altman’ on X last week. That’s where the name for this script came from.

ram-altman is a free bash script available on GitHub. It runs natively on Linux. On Windows, you can use WSL to run it, but someone has ported it for PowerShell too. It won’t run on Mac, but Altman and his minions would find it inconvenient, at best, to remove your soldered-down Apple RAM.

#10378. Top 6 AI Image Generators in 2026: Features, Strengths, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A designer who once spent an afternoon searching stock libraries can now describe the picture they want and have four versions in under a minute. AI image generators have moved from curiosity to everyday tool for blog headers, product mockups, ad concepts, game art and slide decks. The quality gap between tools has narrowed, but the differences that remain matter a lot: how well each follows a detailed prompt, whether it can render readable text, how it handles edits, and what you are allowed to do with the result.

This guide compares six of the best-known options, what each does well and where each falls short. Models, plans and limits change often, so check each vendor's own pages before you commit to a workflow.

OpenArt’s AI image generator puts several image models behind one interface. Its generator page lists models including GPT Image 2.5, Grok Imagine, Qwen Image 3, Nano Banana 2 and Seedream 5.0 Pro, and lets you create images from text prompts or reference photos. It also lists tools for camera angle control, upscaling, collages and a Character Builder for keeping characters consistent across scenes, with 4K export.

Best for: Creators who want to compare several models on the same prompt without juggling separate subscriptions and accounts.

Limitations: The page offers a free start but does not state credit amounts or plan details, and it does not address commercial use, so check the pricing and licensing pages before using results for client work. Output quality depends on which model you pick.

OpenAI's image generation in ChatGPT works inside the chat interface, which makes iteration natural: generate an image, then ask for changes in plain language. OpenAI highlights accurate text rendering, the ability to follow prompts with many distinct objects, and consistent characters across multi-turn edits. You can also upload reference images and have the model transform them. It handles both photorealistic and stylized looks.

Best for: Marketers and writers who want to describe, refine and re-describe an image without learning a new tool.

Limitations: OpenAI notes that tall images such as posters can be cropped too tightly, and that dense small text, multilingual text and precise graphs can still go wrong. Images can take up to a minute to render. All generated images carry C2PA metadata identifying their source.

Gemini's image generation runs on Google's Nano Banana models. Google highlights character consistency, text rendering in many languages, combining several photos, precise editing and 2K resolution. A free tier covers image generation and editing, while the most advanced model requires a Google AI plan. You start from the "Create images" option in the Gemini app, then prompt or upload a picture to edit.

Best for: People who need to keep the same subject across several images, or who want to edit existing photos quickly.

Limitations: Usage limits apply, and the strongest model is behind a paid plan. Images include an invisible SynthID watermark and a visible AI label, which is good for transparency but may not suit every use.

Midjourney is an independent research lab known for images with a strong, painterly look. Its official site points users to a sign-up flow and to a Discord community and help center for support. Many creators choose it for concept art, mood boards and illustration where style matters more than literal accuracy.

Best for: Artists, art directors and game designers who want rich, stylized results and enjoy exploring variations.

Limitations: I could not confirm current pricing, trial terms or feature details from Midjourney's public pages, so check them yourself before subscribing. The workflow can feel less like a normal app than the others on this list, and it is not the easiest route for precise, text-heavy graphics.

Adobe Firefly is built for professionals who already work in Creative Cloud. Adobe states that its Firefly models are commercially safe, trained on licensed Adobe Stock and public domain content, and outputs carry Content Credentials that show AI involvement. It offers text-to-image, image-to-image, sketch-to-image and style transfer, plus editing tools such as background removal, object removal, upscaling and generative expand. It connects with Photoshop, Illustrator, Lightroom and Adobe Express, and also lets you use partner models from other companies.

Best for: Designers and brand teams who need clear licensing and want AI inside the apps they already use.

Limitations: Plans work on a credit system, so heavy use adds up. Adobe lists a free tier with limited daily generations and paid plans starting at about $9.99 a month, but confirm current terms on its site.

Ideogram built its reputation on a problem that trips up many generators: readable text. Its current models are presented as producing clean typography in different layouts, with attention to prompt fidelity and reliable editing. It also offers an API with access to a catalog of models, and has released open weights for one earlier model for local use and customization under its license.

Best for: Posters, social graphics, logo concepts and anything where words are part of the image.

Limitations: The pages I checked did not describe a free plan, so look at current plans before relying on it. Like every generator, it still needs a human to proofread the text it produces.

Start with the job. If you want to compare several models side by side, try OpenArt. If you want to talk your way to a finished image, try ChatGPT or Gemini. If you need images that will sit inside a branded design workflow, Firefly is the safer fit. If you are chasing a distinctive look, Midjourney deserves a trial. If text must be perfect, test Ideogram.

Run the same prompt in two or three tools before paying. Differences in composition, hands, faces and text show up quickly, and they matter more than any feature list.

Reviews only go so far, because your own use case is what counts. Pick one realistic job, for example a 16:9 header image for a blog post about web performance, and run the same prompt through two or three tools. Write the prompt once and paste it unchanged, so the comparison is fair:

Then score each result on five questions: Did it follow every instruction? Are there odd details in hands, edges or objects? Is any text spelled correctly? Does it still look good when cropped to a square? Could you license it for your project? Ten minutes of this teaches more than any feature list, and it shows how much editing each tool needs before you can publish.

Most readers of this guide will put these images on web pages, where a few habits matter more than the generator you choose.

There is no single best AI image generator. The right pick depends on whether you value conversation, style, licensing, control or typography. Test two or three on a real project, compare the output, and keep a human in charge of the final choice. Whatever you generate, review it for errors and label AI-made images clearly where your audience would expect it.

Are AI-generated images free to use commercially? It depends on the provider and your plan. Read the licensing terms for the tool you use, and consider vendors that publish how their models were trained if you work for clients.

Which AI image generator is best for text in images? Ideogram and ChatGPT both highlight text rendering, and Gemini lists it as a feature. Test each with your own wording and proofread the result.

Do AI images have watermarks? Some do. OpenAI adds C2PA metadata, Google adds an invisible SynthID watermark and a visible label, and Adobe attaches Content Credentials.

Can I use more than one image model without separate accounts? Yes. Platforms such as OpenArt put several models in one interface, which makes side-by-side testing easier. Check the licensing terms for each model you use.

Should I add alt text to AI-generated images? Yes, when the image carries meaning. Describe what it shows or why it is there. Purely decorative images can use an empty alt attribute, following the W3C guidance.

#10375. Step by Step guide: Build a Voice Agent That Never Goes Silent using ADK

A guide to streaming tools, non-blocking function calls, and response scheduling in the Agent Development Kit (ADK) Gemini Live API Toolkit.

In text chat, a 13-second database query or RAG pipeline shows a loading spinner. In a live voice conversation, 13 seconds of blocking tool execution produces 13 seconds of dead air, causing users to assume the call dropped and hang up.

You cannot always make backend queries sub-second, so the fix must be architectural: keep the agent conversational while slow tools run in the background. Rather than forcing you to wire up custom client-side WebSocket event loops to manage asynchronous tool state, the open-source ADK and gemini-3.8-live let you implement non-blocking voice tools natively in Python. In our benchmark sales-report workflow on gemini-3.8-live, switching from a blocking tool to a streaming tool cut the time to the agent's first words from 14.4 seconds to 1.2 seconds.

This guide covers how to use streaming tools, non-blocking FunctionTool definitions, and response_scheduling to build responsive, natural voice agents.

Watch the 1-minute ADK Live AI Concierge demo video to hear scheduled responses in action:

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Implementing non-blocking tool calls in ADK provides several immediate user experience benefits:

In standard agent development, a tool is defined as an async def function that completes its work and returns a single value:

When passed directly to an Agent in ADK, this function creates a blocking tool call. During a Gemini Live API bidirectional voice session (run_live), the execution loop awaits the tool's return before generating further model turns.

If query_sales_database takes 13 seconds, the agent produces 13 seconds of complete silence. Prompting instructions such as "tell the user you are working on it" cannot resolve this, because the model's turn is blocked waiting for the function response payload.

To keep the conversation fluid, you can choose between two main non-blocking patterns depending on the desired user experience.

When an operation consists of meaningful stages (such as connecting to a database, aggregating records, and formatting results), a streaming tool lets the agent narrate progress as it happens.

In ADK, turning a standard tool into a streaming tool requires changing only return to yield. By defining the function as an AsyncGenerator, ADK automatically marks the tool declaration as NON_BLOCKING and returns an immediate status acknowledgement to the model.

Continuous progress commentary is not always appropriate. If a user asks for a report and immediately pivots to another topic, an agent that continually interrupts with status updates can feel intrusive.

When the wait has no story to narrate, run the task quietly in the background.

An effective way to get an immediate verbal acknowledgment without ongoing progress chatter is an async generator that yields only once, upon completion:

Because this is an async generator, ADK issues an immediate pending response. The agent promptly acknowledges the request ("I'm pulling that report together for you now"), remains silent during computation, and presents the figures once ready or when the user finishes speaking.

If you prefer to keep standard coroutines (async def ... -> dict), wrap the function in a FunctionTool and configure response_scheduling:

Understanding the Trade-Off: When using response_scheduling with a return-once coroutine, the model receives no payload until the entire function completes. As a result, the model typically does not utter an initial verbal acknowledgment unless the user continues speaking. This pattern is ideal when the user immediately continues with other questions and you want the result woven into the dialogue at the next natural pause.

The response_scheduling attribute controls when the model reacts to an incoming tool response. WHEN_IDLE never cuts into a turn in progress: if the model is still speaking or the user is still talking, the result waits until that turn ends, and the model responds at the next natural pause. ADK supports three scheduling behaviors:

In benchmark sessions with gemini-3.8-live:

Execution shape (streaming vs. single-return) and delivery timing (response_scheduling) are independent and can be composed. Setting response_scheduling on a streaming tool stamps every partial update with that behavior.

Furthermore, in ADK 2.8 and newer, streaming tools can dynamically specify scheduling per update by yielding types.FunctionResponse:

The rule of thumb: If the wait has a story, stream it. If the wait is just a wait, run it in the background and let the result land when idle.

Non-blocking tools do not reduce backend execution time: a 13-second query still takes 13 seconds. What changes is the conversational presence of your agent.

A web application that takes ten seconds to load is perceived as slow; a conversational voice agent that goes silent for ten seconds is perceived as broken. By pairing ADK's streaming tools and flexible response scheduling with the real-time capabilities of gemini-3.8-live, you can build voice assistants that remain attentive, natural, and present throughout complex operations.

Try it yourself: the interactive demo runs the same 13-second query through every tool pattern and response_scheduling mode on gemini-3.8-live, so you can hear the difference.
