China's 800MW AI campus takes on Stargate with wind power, batteries and 800V DC

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

China is building an 800MW AI data center campus in Inner Mongolia that could challenge the economics of America's Stargate project, according to a new report from SemiAnalysis. Envision has built the first 120 MW of Galaxy AI data center campus in less than a year, a cost the researchers put at just one-fifth of comparable US projects, after visiting the site in September.

But getting the buildings up is only half of the battle. AI data centers need enormous amounts of electricity, and Envision is taking an unusual route to secure it— building its own wind farms and battery storage alongside the computing facility.

While US developers are turning to on-site gas turbines to work around grid constraints, Envision is building its data center alongside wind farms, battery storage, and dedicated power infrastructure. Galaxy is located in Ulanqab, Inner Mongolia, and is being developed across four phases for two unnamed internet companies. The completed campus is planned to provide up to 800MW of IT capacity by the end of 2027, putting it closer to the approximately 820MW of critical IT capacity at the Stargate campus in Abilene, Texas.

Envision is building its own power supply for Galaxy rather than leaving everything to the local grid. So far, the company has installed more than 100GW of wind turbines and wants its wind farms to provide over 60% of the electricity needed by the campus’s first two phases. Those farms, located about 80 kilometers apart, have a combined capacity of 420MW. That's well above the 240MW load expected from the first two phases, although wind turbines rarely get to run flat out. Ulanqab’s wind capacity factor is between 355 and 40%, so Envision is adding four-hour battery storage to keep the electricity coming when the wind dies down.

Power equipment is proving rather hard to come by for some US data center builders. We have previously reported on turbine shortages that have pushed delivery estimates to 2030, while SpaceX is looking to manufacture its own turbine blades for its gas-powered AI facilities. Envision, meanwhile, is putting wind generation and battery storage right alongside its servers.

The power hardware inside the Galaxy is worth a look, too. Semianalyss visited a 5MW hall where solid-state transformers take 10kV AC electricity and convert it directly into 800V DC for the server racks. That's a fairly substantial departure from conventional data-center power distribution, which typically involves several intermediate conversion stages. “In one 5MW hall, we saw the 800V system at work,” the researchers wrote. They explained that “Solid-state transformers (SSTs) take 10kV AC from the grid and convert it straight to 800V DC for the racks.”

The design removes several intermediate components found in conventional data center power systems, including low voltage transformers, uninterruptible power supplies and rectifiers. Fewer conversion stages can reduce equipment requirements and energy losses, although SemiAnalysis did not provide a measured efficiency improvement for Galaxy’s pilot. The 5MW installation is only a small part of the campus, but it puts the technology into operation at a live facility. Envision, however, plans an even larger deployment in the next construction phases, a move that aligns with Nvidia's push for 800V DC distribution in future high-density AI systems.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Galaxy's campus uses a cooling arrangement in which liquid-cooled and air-cooled servers share a single water loop, eliminating the need for conventional central chiller plants. That matters as AI servers generate more heat and require increasingly sophisticated cooling systems. Removing central chillers can simplify infrastructure, although the design depends on local climate conditions and the facility's ability to reject heat through its water loop. China has previously experimented with other unconventional cooling methods, including a wind-powered underwater datacenter off Shanghai that uses surrounding seawater to help cool its servers.

That said, Galaxy is the first major project in Invision's wider Inner Mongolia datacentre plans, which Semianalysis says add up to 3GW, with the rest of the campus still a busy construction site. The surrounding Chahar High-tech Zone is also expanding rapidly, with nearly 30,000 construction workers spread across 22 data center projects, according to a local report cited by the researchers.

The wider Chinese expansion is also attracting some of the world's largest technology companies. Alibaba, Huawei and ByteDance are among the firms expanding infrastructure in the region, which benefits from relatively cheap electricity, available land and abundant wind resources. However access to electricity and datacenter buildings does not automatically translate to available AI computing power. China's access to advanced accelerators remains a separate constraint, particularly as US export restrictions limit its ability to acquire Nvidia’s most powerful chips.

#10370. Lawyer builds private AI server with eight used Nvidia V100 GPUs

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The builder estimates 50% to 60% of their AI work can now skip frontier models, running on two office circuits.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A lawyer posted on Reddit about their private AI server built from eight used 2018-era Nvidia Tesla V100 data center GPUs so case files never have to leave the office. With two 4-card SXM2 NVLink boards, four PEX switches, and SlimSAS cables everywhere — plus one burnt power connector — the builder now runs the open DeepSeek V4.1 Flash model with every expert in GPU memory, at up to 215 tokens per second. The four-GPU boards cost about $2,400 apiece when the builder bought them.

The eight Tesla V100-SXM2-32GB cards, each with 32GB of HBM2 at 900 GB/s, plug into SXM2, Nvidia’s mezzanine socket for data center GPUs, rather than PCIe slots, and are split across two baseboards. Inside each board, all four cards are NVLinked — NVLink is Nvidia’s direct GPU-to-GPU link — for 128GB per board, but the two boards only communicate over PCIe. Each board connects to the host through two PEX (PLX) switch cards over SlimSAS cables, four switches in all, with each switch handling two GPUs at PCIe 3.0 x8 speeds over its own x16 uplink.

The given prices are already in the past, though: used V100 SXM2 32GB cards are now listed on eBay for about $900, more than double the roughly $400 apiece from the post, and listings suggest prices are still climbing. The system also already has some other necessary components: a Gigabyte Threadripper Pro motherboard with a 12-core 3945WX CPU to match, 8x 32GB DDR4 RDIMM, one NUMA node, and two PSUs, 2,800 W in total, are on separate regular office circuits. This is a real-world case for used data center hardware as a cheap route to getting lots of fast VRAM together in one place.

DeepSeek V4.1 Flash is compressed to mostly 2 bits here, and its weights still total 248.8GB. The model’s “experts,” the parts that do most of the work for each word, live entirely in GPU memory, while about 110GB of lookup tables, called Engram tables, stay in the system’s regular RAM. Fetching from RAM costs about 0.12 milliseconds per step, which the builder says barely affects the speed numbers.

The first half of the model’s layers (0–19) runs on the first SXM2 board and the second half (20–39) on the second. Each board handles its heavy traffic over its own NVLink, so only one hand-off per step has to cross the slower PCIe link between the boards. In an earlier post, the builder found that splitting every layer across all eight cards made speeds “fall off a cliff” because of that PCIe hop.

Performance is surprisingly good for a model this size. Code comes out fastest, at up to 215 tokens per second, because a small draft model built into DeepSeek guesses five tokens ahead, and code is easy to guess. Legal writing, which is what the server is for, runs at up to 118 tokens per second.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Long documents take longer to get going: the model reads prompts at about 2,200 tokens per second, so at 128K tokens, the first token arrives after about a minute. It can accept up to 524K tokens of context. Three days before the post, the same model on four of these cards managed just 36–38 tokens per second. The jump came with the second board, which could presumably double performance under ideal circumstances, and the builder credits custom kernels and prefill work for the rest. See the table for the full breakdown.

Builder’s measurements, Nov. 5; greedy decoding, 1,024 tokens out, mean of two runs.

It wasn’t as simple as hooking everything up and turning on the power. The host reset itself twice with a fatal PCIe error on the link to one GPU board, both times with a higher power limit and uncapped GPU clocks. In this state, the cards drew 192–232 W, past the 180 W limit. The best guess, although “not proven,” is that a board stops responding during load spikes.

The resets stopped after the builder capped the clocks and removed one board whose power connector had a burn mark; “Check your connectors, people.” Each card is now capped at 150 W, half of its 300 W rating, with the graphics clock capped at 1522 MHz. A 200 W cap brought no appreciable performance gains, according to tests.

The system memory hit uncorrected ECC errors, too, which went away after the builder dropped the RAM from DDR4-3200 to DDR4-2666. One GPU was connected at x4 instead of x8 at last check, and the builder plans to reseat it; one commenter traced the same kind of problem on their own V100 to a bad cable.

The pain was worth the payoff as an estimated 50–60% of the lawyer’s existing LLM workflows can now run on the box instead of frontier models. Helping bridge the gap is a second DeepSeek server on two DGX Sparks, where the two systems hand work off to each other. The setup does appear to save money, at least when compared to API pricing, although the builder says it’s not really faster. However, it will protect client data “once it is self contained, which is the goal.”

The builder added that the V100 box might also get a 240 V circuit. Beyond power, the system is also on borrowed time with the Nvidia R580 driver branch, which is the last to support Volta. For now, the lawyer admits that it’s mostly a hobby, but in the future it may be a business idea. The next step is to handle two or three requests at once, something built and checked on a test system but not yet on the server.

#10368. Which MCM Has the Best Revshare? A Developer’s Guide to Evaluating Ad Partners

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An ad partner’s revenue share looks easy to compare: keeping 90% sounds better than keeping 80%. But the percentage does not tell a developer what will happen when the integration reaches production. Extra JavaScript, unstable ad slots, opaque reporting, and difficult rollbacks can change the value of an apparently attractive deal.

For teams maintaining an advertising-funded website, choosing a Multiple Customer Management (MCM) partner is partly an engineering decision. The integration affects page templates, consent handling, third-party requests, account access, and monitoring. Those changes deserve the same scrutiny as a new analytics SDK or payment integration.

This guide explains how to compare revenue shares on a consistent basis, review integration requirements, and test an ad setup without sacrificing usability. The goal is a repeatable evaluation process, rather than a universal winner.

Google Ad Manager’s MCM system lets a parent publisher manage a child publisher’s account or delegated inventory. The commercial agreement defines the partner’s services and compensation. Before comparing offers, ask which revenue streams the quoted percentage covers and whether the reporting figure is before or after that fee.

Use three separate lines in your evaluation spreadsheet:

Revenue base: record the amount to which the partner’s percentage applies. Advertiser spending, exchange-reported revenue, and publisher earnings are different figures. Do not treat them as interchangeable.

Partner compensation: identify the percentage, fixed charges, and any optional services. Ask whether the same terms apply to every demand source and ad format.

Adjustments: distinguish estimated revenue from finalized earnings, including invalid-traffic deductions and other corrections. Document when adjustments appear and which party bears them.

A common mistake is applying the partner fee twice because a dashboard already displays net revenue. Label each exported metric before using it in a comparison.

The two delegation models have different implications for access, reporting, and payments. Google’s documentation is the starting point for checking the proposed setup.

Manage Account gives the parent edit access within the child’s account while the child retains access and permissions. Payment facilitation can allocate eligible earnings from the child’s primary Ad Exchange account, but it does not automatically cover every third-party revenue stream. Confirm the invitation’s payment percentage and the separate commercial agreement.

Manage Inventory lets the parent manage delegated inventory in its own account, without access to the child account. Reporting for that inventory sits in the parent account. Google pays the parent for the relevant inventory, and the parent pays the child according to their agreement. Ask how you will obtain reports and reconcile payments.

Before implementation, document who can change tags, ad units, consent settings, and demand configuration. Agree on access for debugging and exporting historical data. A developer should be able to trace a production request to its configuration owner.

Use this table as a request-for-information checklist. It does not rank providers or assert that any named service supports a particular implementation. Obtain current terms and technical documentation for the product you are considering.

Apply the same standards to every proposal. The following examples show what a developer should ask during evaluation; each question is relevant across providers.

MonetizeMore offers publisher monetization products, including PubGuru and Traffic Cop. When evaluating its proposal, connect the commercial discussion to concrete integration questions: which scripts will load, who owns the configuration, and how will reported earnings be reconciled?

Request a sample report showing the revenue base, partner fee, and deductions as separate fields. Check whether the proposed plan includes every service shown in the sales demonstration.

Ask for a written ownership map covering Ad Manager access, tag deployment, configuration changes, and incident response. Identify who can restore the previous setup if delivery fails.

During a trial, compare representative templates and devices. Record script requests and main-thread activity alongside net revenue, using the same measurement definitions for the existing setup.

Decision criterion: choose on measured results and documented responsibilities, rather than assuming that a quoted split predicts final earnings.

For any proposal involving a header-bidding wrapper, including one from Setupad, ask how the wrapper fits into your existing page lifecycle.

Review bidder configuration, auction timeouts, and when the ad server receives targeting information. A missing bid and an unfilled ad slot are different problems.

Check whether your team can inspect the active configuration and reproduce an issue on a test page. Agree on who approves changes to bidders or timeout values.

Measure the complete sequence: consent readiness, auction start, bid responses, ad request, and rendering. Avoid attributing every delay to the same component.

Decision criterion: the implementation should be observable enough to explain failures, with commercial terms verified separately.

When assessing Publisher Collective or another managed partner, establish how the proposed setup could be tested on a limited, agreed scope.

Ask whether rollout can be restricted to selected templates or an eligible traffic cohort. Do not assume partial testing is available until the partner confirms it.

Record device, geography, page type, and consent state so differences in traffic mix do not masquerade as a revenue improvement.

Keep a dated deployment log connecting each configuration version with the reporting period.

Decision criterion: a measured comparison should separate implementation changes from changes in audience or demand.

A proposal from AdPushup or another optimization provider may involve changes to ad placement. Review the actual proposed changes with both design and engineering.

Check spacing, responsive behavior, keyboard navigation, and whether sticky placements obscure content or controls.

Reserve space for expected ad dimensions and test what happens when no ad is returned.

Compare layout stability before and after the change.

Decision criterion: additional earnings should be assessed alongside usability and performance, with explicit acceptance criteria.

For an Ezoic proposal, as for any managed platform, start with the exact integration method offered to your site.

List required changes to scripts, plugins, caching, DNS, or delivery infrastructure, if any. Do not assume that every deployment uses the same method.

Test interactions with your consent manager, content security policy, caching rules, and existing analytics.

Document how to remove the integration and restore the prior configuration.

Decision criterion: the operational cost should be visible before the first production rollout.

When reviewing a Monumetric proposal, establish which implementation work belongs to your team and which belongs to the provider.

Ask who creates ad slots, updates templates, and validates responsive sizes.

Request a support escalation process with the diagnostic information required for an incident: URL, timestamp, device, consent state, and affected slot.

Confirm onboarding costs and payment terms in writing.

Decision criterion: routine changes and production failures should have a clear owner.

Chrome DevTools helps trace third-party requests in the Network panel and investigate long tasks in the Performance panel. Capture a baseline before deployment, then repeat the same navigation and interaction sequence after deployment. Use comparable device and network settings.

Google Publisher Console helps investigate Google Publisher Tag ad slots and delivery issues. For a Prebid-based setup, use Prebid’s debugging tools to inspect the auction path. These tools answer different questions: whether the auction behaved as expected and whether the ad server delivered the requested slot.

PageSpeed Insights provides performance diagnostics; distinguish its lab results from available field data. Add web-vitals to your own monitoring when you need measurements associated with a deployment or cohort. Track LCP, INP, and CLS alongside revenue instead of treating a single Lighthouse score as the complete user experience.

Reserve responsive space for ad slots before content arrives, and investigate shifts when empty slots collapse. SitePoint’s guide to web performance optimization explains related techniques for loading JavaScript, reducing layout work, and managing long tasks.

With the same traffic volume, revenue definition, and deduction rate, the break-even increase in pre-partner revenue is: (current publisher share ÷ new publisher share) − 1. Express shares consistently as fractions or percentages. The table below shows the required lift; it is not a forecast these thresholds only describe the arithmetic. Different ad density, audience mix, deduction rates, or reporting bases can invalidate the comparison. Normalize to the same denominator, such as revenue per thousand pageviews, and compare matched periods or properly assigned concurrent cohorts.

Assume two hypothetical setups each receive one million pageviews. Here, RPM means revenue per thousand pageviews before the partner fee. For this simplified example, deductions reduce that revenue before the publisher share is applied.

Partner A: $10 pre-partner RPM × 1,000 groups of pageviews × 0.90 after deductions × 0.90 publisher share = $8,100 net revenue.

Partner B: $12 pre-partner RPM × 1,000 groups of pageviews × 0.98 after deductions × 0.80 publisher share = $9,408 net revenue.

The difference is $1,308 for the period despite Partner B’s lower share. These are illustrative inputs, not provider performance claims. Faster payment improves cash-flow timing; it does not, by itself, increase the revenue earned.

Use Playwright with mocked responses or test creatives in a controlled environment to check slow loading, empty slots, blocked scripts, and navigation between routes. Verify that the page remains usable when the ad system fails. Automated tests should not click live advertisements or generate artificial monetized traffic.

Separate unit tests for configuration logic, integration tests for component boundaries, and browser tests for user-visible behavior. SitePoint’s explanation of unit, functional, and integration testing provides a useful foundation. Define rollback criteria before launch, keep the control configuration available, and review finalized revenue alongside performance data before expanding the deployment.

There is no universal answer from the percentage alone. Compare current written offers using the same revenue base, then evaluate finalized net earnings, performance, access, and implementation effort.

Not necessarily. Reports may differ in timezone, currency, transaction coverage, fee treatment, and finalization date. Reconcile those definitions before treating a discrepancy as an integration bug.

Ask how renewals, volume tiers, optional services, and rate changes are handled. Record the applicable terms with the configuration and reporting period used for your evaluation.

No. If deductions and the revenue base are otherwise comparable, an 80% share needs 12.5% more pre-partner revenue to match a 90% share. Measure whether that lift exists rather than assuming it.

Do not treat any protection claim as a guarantee. Ask how suspected invalid traffic is handled, what evidence is available, and how later adjustments are reflected in your reports and payments.

Choose the setup that produces verifiable net value while meeting the site’s performance and operational requirements. A good evaluation leaves your team with reproducible measurements, understandable reports, and a practical rollback plan—not just a favorable percentage.

#10366. Ray-Ban Meta Audio review: Great specs, shame about the price

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses attempt to curb the controversy by removing the cameras, and while they’re still an excellent product, the price makes them harder to recommend.

This has been a bit of a rollercoaster year for Meta wearables. Meta’s VR division shrank considerably earlier this year, then later announced a massively redesigned follow-up that’ll launch next year. And while Meta has debuted dozens of new smart glasses styles throughout the year that have sold quite well, the company can’t seem to get away from controversy. That’s where Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses come in.

Now, that doesn’t mean Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses aren’t worthwhile. They feature a much wider prescription range (-12 to +6) than the majority of Meta smart glasses models, which often cap out at -6 to +6. The battery life is also significantly better thanks to the lack of a camera and other improvements in the hardware. And, of course, if you want a pair of AI glasses but don’t want the camera, these are the only options from Meta.

If I were to choose between the two available models, I’d pick the Clubmaster style every time. Not only do they look better overall and feature the thinnest frames of any Meta smart glasses, but they also have substantially better nosepads that are adjustable and can be easily replaced.

The Burbank model looks just fine, but the nosepads aren’t the same, and the glasses feel more rigid overall (which I find less comfortable). Both models come in lots of colors with many different lens options, including clear, tinted, polarized, Transitions, and prescription.

Both models feature a touchpad on the outside of the right temple arm, as well as a small, circular button up top to activate Meta AI. You can alternatively use the “Hey, Meta” keywords to activate Meta AI. The button and touchpad can be disabled if you like. Only the touchpad has an alternative function, and that alternative function only controls music playback via Spotify or Amazon Music. Other Meta glasses offer far more options.

Both styles are the lightest Meta glasses available. Most Meta smart glasses with cameras weigh in around the 54g mark. Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses weigh just 44g. They’re not as light as Even Realities G2, RayNeo iO, or MemoMind One, but 44g is still considered pretty light for smart glasses.

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses reuse the case from Ray-Ban Meta Gen 2, which looks more like a traditional Ray-Ban case rather than the foldable one that ships with Meta Ray-Ban Display or the 2026 Meta Camera Glasses. I don’t have an issue with Ray-Ban Meta Audio’s case at all, but I really enjoy the foldable one and wish it were included.

Audio playback on these is great, and while the speakers are identical to all other Meta glasses products, using them as spatial surround sound “headphones” while watching a movie with surround sound is a fantastic experience. They also have spatial audio recording and truly excellent noise cancellation thanks to the 6-mic setup.

But the lack of the newer processor from Ray-Ban Meta Gen 3 means the glasses don’t support the new hearing aid function. To me, that’s a massive missed opportunity since these are audio-only glasses. When I asked Meta about it, they only said it wasn’t available on Ray-Ban Meta Audio for now, so this problem could be fixed in an upcoming update.

And that’s actually where Meta’s glasses might shine brightest: software updates. The company has been exemplary with update cadence on all of its products, and its smart glasses constantly get new, innovative features that surprise and delight. While I’m a little underwhelmed with the number of features available on these glasses today, this product will likely look quite different a year from now, a fact that’s backed up by years of Meta significantly improving product feature sets over time.

If you buy these glasses today, know that you’re mainly investing in them for the future. They’re fine enough for today’s usage, especially if you’re interested in a hands-free way to use Meta AI products like Muse or just want an open-ear way to listen to music and take phone calls, but Meta’s penchant for continually adding new features is, undoubtedly, going to be the highlight for everyone who ever buys them.

#10365. Memory shortage spurs smartphone makers to melt used memory off old devices, then re-solder it onto new products

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The smartphone industry is expected to see a spike in reballed memory adoption. According to a report by global market intelligence and research firm TrendForce, the current penetration rate is estimated at around 3–4%, with reballed memory primarily used in entry-level and budget smartphone models. However, this figure is expected to rise to 7–9% in 2027.

Reballed memory is recycled memory salvaged from older or discarded devices. The term reball comes from the process itself, in which the solder balls on a used memory chip are first melted so the chip can be removed from the old device, then re-soldered to a new device, thus allowing the component to be reused rather than discarded. You can see the process in action in the video below.

This offers manufacturers a cheaper alternative to sourcing new memory chips, potentially helping them keep production costs under control. Currently, reballed 4GB LPDDR4X memory modules make up the majority of such components on the market, and they are used in 4G smartphones priced below CNY 1,299 (approximately $200). This tactic is becoming increasingly important for smartphone makers, as customers are particularly sensitive to price increases in the budget segment. This leaves limited room to raise retail prices without affecting demand. Using reballed memory can help offset rising bill of materials (BOM) costs while allowing brands to keep entry-level devices affordable.

TrendForce expects DRAM prices to rise even further in the first quarter of 2027, which may put additional pressure on smartphone manufacturers. As a result, the use of recycled memory and other lower-specification components could gradually expand beyond the entry-level segment and into more premium smartphones. What is currently a cost-saving measure for budget devices could eventually become a broader strategy for manufacturers looking to manage expenses amid a prolonged period of high memory prices.The rising cost of memory is also pushing PC enthusiasts and hardware hobbyists to explore unconventional ways to reuse existing DRAM chips. Recently, an electronics hobbyist converted a relatively inexpensive $120 DDR5 SO-DIMM kit into a desktop memory kit valued at around $1,000. The project involved salvaging an SK hynix 2x 16GB DDR5-5600 SODIMM kit purchased from Facebook Marketplace and reballing the chips onto custom desktop DIMM PCBs, with the resulting modules reportedly capable of reaching DDR5-7800 speeds.

Converting laptop memory into desktop DIMMs is technically demanding and requires considerable expertise and specialized equipment. Similarly, reballing memory for use in smartphones is far from straightforward, particularly given the tiny solder connections in modern BGA (Ball Grid Array) packages. Nevertheless, both developments highlight how manufacturers, repair specialists, and hardware enthusiasts are exploring alternatives to reuse existing memory to offset rising costs and limited availability.
